## 2026-08-12 — Rebuilt as an Astro site (not loose markdown)

- User decided the plain-markdown-folder approach wasn't the right medium — the point is to teach in an engaging way, not to produce something as flat as a `.txt`. Chose Astro + MDX + Tailwind (recommended over plain HTML) and bun as the package manager/runtime.
- Scaffolded Astro 7 in place, added `@astrojs/mdx`, Tailwind v4 (`@tailwindcss/vite`) + `@tailwindcss/typography`, and `mermaid`. Moved all 16 track folders under `src/content/` (required by Astro content collections).
- Built `scripts/generate-roadmap-data.mjs`: parses `ROADMAP.md`'s per-track tables, assigns a stable kebab-case slug to any row missing one (rewriting `ROADMAP.md` with an explicit `Slug` column so slugs never drift on re-run), and emits `src/data/roadmap.json` (+ `src/data/roadmap.ts` typed wrapper). `ROADMAP.md` stays the human-edited source of truth; the JSON is generated and gets regenerated via `bun run generate:roadmap`.
- Built the rendering pipeline: `src/content.config.ts` (glob-loaded `curriculum` collection), `src/lib/curriculum.ts` (groups raw entries into per-unit `{l1, l2, l3, l3Parts}`), `Layout.astro` (nav + client-side Mermaid rendering of fenced ```mermaid blocks), `LevelTabs.astro` (L1/L2/L3 tab switcher), `ProgressToggle.astro` (localStorage-backed "mark as done"), and pages for home (`/`), `/roadmap`, `/[track]/`, `/[track]/[unit]/`.
- Verified end-to-end with a throwaway `_smoketest` content fixture (since deleted) via `bun run build` + `astro preview --background`, checked live in Chrome. Found and fixed two real bugs in the process: (1) the glob loader lowercases generated entry ids, so the id-parser in `curriculum.ts` needed case-insensitive matching against `L1-`/`L2-`/`L3-` prefixes; (2) `LevelTabs` and `ProgressToggle` only initialized on the `astro:page-load` event, which never fires without a `ClientRouter`/View Transitions — fixed by also calling init directly on script execution.
- Also learned: `bun run dev` (file-watching) reliably fails to start within Astro's 30s timeout on this `/mnt/c/...` WSL-over-Windows path — `bun run build` + `astro preview --background` is the dependable local loop, now documented in `CLAUDE.md` and `README.md`.
- No study content has been written yet — this session was entirely infrastructure. All units remain `planned`.
- Updated `CLAUDE.md` (full "Tech stack" section, new file/folder naming under `src/content/`, session workflow now includes running `generate:roadmap` and a real build check), `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` (new "Structure on disk" reflecting the Astro layout), `README.md` (run instructions).
- Next: pick the first unit and track together with the user — this time it will actually render as a page, not just a markdown file.

## 2026-08-12 — Added lint/format/typecheck/content-validation/test guardrails + scaffold script

- User asked for security/lint/test measures and rules that are always read, so that starting a new topic from their side is always easy and never leads to an inconsistent state.
- Added ESLint (flat config, `eslint.config.mjs`, TS + Astro + Node-aware for `scripts/`), Prettier (`prettier-plugin-astro`), `astro check` for types, and `scripts/validate-content.mjs` — which cross-checks `ROADMAP.md`, `src/data/roadmap.json`, and the real files under `src/content/` for drift (missing roadmap rows, stale generated data, status/content mismatches, missing frontmatter).
- Added `scripts/new-unit.mjs` (`bun run new:unit <track> <unit-number-or-slug>`) — scaffolds a unit's three level files with correct frontmatter and flips its `ROADMAP.md` status to `in-progress` in one command, so the user (or Claude) always starts from the same known-good shape.
- Added `bun test` coverage (`bun:test`) for the two places a silent parsing bug would be hardest to spot by eye: the roadmap slug generator (`scripts/generate-roadmap-data.test.mjs`) and the content-entry-id parser (`src/lib/curriculum.test.ts`). Had to refactor `curriculum.ts` to type-only import `astro:content` and dynamic-import `getCollection` at call time, since the virtual module doesn't resolve under plain `bun test`.
- Added `bun run check` aggregating generate:roadmap → lint → format:check → typecheck → validate:content → test → build; documented in `CLAUDE.md` as mandatory before ending any content/code session, replacing the earlier "just run build" guidance.
- Found and fixed two real bugs while dogfooding all of this: (1) `generate-roadmap-data.mjs` wrote `src/data/roadmap.json` before reformatting `ROADMAP.md` with Prettier, so `ROADMAP.md`'s mtime ended up newer than the JSON it just generated from, causing `validate:content` to always report the data as stale — reordered so Prettier runs before the JSON write; (2) `new-unit.mjs`'s status-flip regex assumed single-space table cells, but Prettier pads columns for alignment — fixed to match on `\s*` like the rest of the parsing already does.
- Verified the whole loop end-to-end with a throwaway scaffolded unit (`web/http-request-response-basics`, since removed, status reverted to `planned`): `new:unit` → `validate:content` correctly flagging staleness → `generate:roadmap` clearing it → cleanup.
- No study content has been written yet. All 218 units remain `planned`.
- Next: pick the first unit and track together with the user.

## 2026-08-12 — First real unit: web/http-request-response-basics (done)

- Wrote the first actual study content, to validate the whole pipeline (structure, tooling, and the site itself) end-to-end with real material rather than a throwaway fixture. `web/01 — HTTP request/response basics`, chosen as the most foundational, broadly-illustrative unit: it exercises a Mermaid diagram, pseudocode, and real runnable code, which most other units will need some subset of.
- L3 code examples (`raw-request.mjs` client, `raw-server.mjs` server, both raw `node:net` sockets with no HTTP library) were actually run under `bun run` and verified against each other locally (not just written and assumed correct) — confirmed `GET /time` and a 404 path both round-trip correctly before considering the unit done.
- Found and fixed a real rendering bug while reviewing the built page in Chrome: Mermaid sequence diagrams mirror actor boxes at the bottom by default, and in our narrow content column that mirrored repeat overlapped the diagram's last note/message instead of getting its own space. Fixed globally via `sequence: { mirrorActors: false }` in `Layout.astro`'s Mermaid init — affects every future Mermaid sequence diagram in the project, not just this unit. (Caught via a user-provided screenshot after a `<br/>`-in-message-text overlap was already fixed — worth remembering: always scroll/screenshot the _entire_ rendered diagram, not just what's initially in viewport, since Mermaid layout bugs can be low enough to be off-screen on first look.)
- Ran `bun run check` clean (0 lint/format/type errors, content validation passed, 9/9 tests, build succeeded) both before and after the Mermaid fix, and visually reviewed all three levels (L1/L2/L3) live in Chrome via `astro preview --background`.
- Marked `web/http-request-response-basics` `done` in `ROADMAP.md` (all three levels complete, not just started) and ran `generate:roadmap`.
- Confirms the structure works for both sides as designed: `new:unit` → write content → `check` → visual review → mark `done` is a clean, repeatable loop with no friction found other than the Mermaid bug above (now fixed for all future units).
- 1/218 units done.
- Next: pick the next unit and track together with the user.

## 2026-08-12 — Second Mermaid fix: note text vertical alignment (user-caught via screenshots)

- After the mirrorActors fix above, the user flagged (with a `/paste` screenshot, then the actual rendered SVG markup) that a note's rect and its text were still visibly misaligned — a problem the visual "does it look okay" pass had missed, and one my browser automation couldn't re-check for a while (Chrome DevTools Protocol screenshot calls started timing out mid-session, on every tab including freshly-created ones — resolved on its own after a few minutes; treated as a transient bridge issue, not a page bug, since Vivaldi itself was responsive the whole time per the user).
- Root cause, from the actual SVG the user pasted: Mermaid's note `<text>` had **both** `dominant-baseline="middle"` and `dy="1em"` set — two different vertical-centering techniques (one for browsers that honor `dominant-baseline`, one for those that don't) stacked instead of chosen between. Browsers that support `dominant-baseline` (Chrome/Vivaldi/Firefox) apply both, so the extra `dy` shifts note text well below the rect's real center.
- First attempt (`transform: translateY(-1em)` on `.noteText`, canceling the dy entirely) was an overcorrection — user confirmed via a second screenshot it was "slightly better but not perfect," text now sitting a bit high. Recomputed from the exact coordinates in the pasted SVG (rect y=179 h=20 → true center 189; text y=184 → dominant-baseline-only center is 184, 5px short of true center) and corrected to `translateY(calc(-1em + 5px))`. User confirmed via a third screenshot this is now correctly centered, both axes.
- Lesson for future units: a `bun run check` pass and even a visual glance are not sufficient to catch sub-pixel-scale Mermaid rendering bugs — they need either a zoomed screenshot of the specific element or, as happened here, the user inspecting actual SVG output. Worth doing a zoomed check on any new diagram type (not just sequence diagrams) before marking a unit `done`.
- Fix lives in `src/styles/global.css` (`.mermaid .noteText { transform: translateY(calc(-1em + 5px)); }`), documented inline with the exact reasoning — applies to every note in every Mermaid sequence diagram project-wide, not just this unit's.

## 2026-08-12 — Built the exercise system: quizzes, sandboxed code exercises, gated "Mark as done", spaced repetition

- User asked for a real testing layer on top of the reading material: self-graded exercises with an "expect" (i.e. real code assertions, not just multiple choice), "Mark as done" only achievable once everything passes with no errors, and periodic automatic un-marking to force a genuine re-exam and catch regressions in retained knowledge. Mid-implementation, the user added two more requirements: every exercise must reveal the solution and the reasoning after any attempt (not just pass/fail), and exercises should be randomized from a pool per topic rather than the same fixed question every time.
- New `exercises` content collection (`src/content.config.ts`): one optional `exercises.json` per unit, `quiz` (multiple choice) and `code` (sandboxed, `expect()`-based) item types. Both types now require an `explanation`; code items also require a `solution` — enforced by `bun run validate:content`, not just convention.
- Items sharing a `poolId` are randomized variants of the same question; `src/lib/curriculum.ts`'s `groupExercisesIntoPools()` groups them, and `ExercisePanel.astro`'s client script picks one at random per render (including after a spaced-repetition reset) and builds its DOM directly (not server-rendered per-variant, since which variant shows has to vary per view).
- Code exercises run in a sandboxed Web Worker (`ExercisePanel.astro`'s `WORKER_SOURCE`) — no DOM/window access, a 3-second timeout guards against infinite loops, and a minimal `expect(actual).toBe/toEqual/toBeTruthy/toThrow()` API is injected. Verified with both a deliberately wrong implementation (correctly fails 2/4 tests with real error messages) and a correct one (4/4 pass) via real browser interaction, not just reading the code.
- `ProgressToggle.astro` rewritten: units with any exercises disable "Mark as done" until every pool has been passed (tracked per-pool in `localStorage`, not per-variant, so gating survives which random variant was shown); units with none keep the old ungated manual toggle. `src/lib/spaced-repetition.ts` schedules re-exams at 7/30/90-day intervals (capped at 90) and, on the next visit past the due date, auto-unmarks the unit **and clears every pool's pass/fail state**, forcing a real re-take with a fresh random variant — verified by manually backdating `nextReviewAt` in localStorage and confirming the reset fired correctly (gating re-locked, all 4 exercise states cleared to null, stage preserved at 1 rather than reset to 0).
- Found and fixed a real bug while testing the toggle: `initProgressToggles()`/`initExercises()` re-run on events like `exercise:graded`, and were re-attaching a fresh click listener to the same button every time without removing the old one — one real click ended up firing the handler multiple times, toggling `done` on then back off. Fixed by splitting each component into a one-time "wire" step (guarded by a `data-wired` flag) and a repeatable "render" step; documented as a required pattern in `CLAUDE.md` for any future interactive component.
- Confirmed GFM tables and Mermaid chart types (`xychart-beta`, etc. — anything beyond sequence diagrams) both render with zero new code, just documentation of usage in `CLAUDE.md`; charts/tables are for illustrating concepts in content, not interactive, per the user's explicit scope answer earlier in this conversation.
- Added real (not placeholder) exercises to `web/http-request-response-basics`: 2 quiz pools (one with 2 randomized variants, one with 1) for L1, 1 quiz for L2, 1 code exercise (`byteLength`, using `TextEncoder` since Workers have no Node `Buffer`) for L3 — all verified working end-to-end in the browser, including the randomized-variant pool (dispatched `exercises:reset` 8 times, saw both variants appear) and the solution/explanation reveal after a failing attempt.
- Updated `CLAUDE.md` with a full "Exercises, 'Mark as done' gating, and spaced repetition" section (format, required fields, pools, gating behavior, the wire/render pattern requirement) and `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` with the "why" (exercises exist because reading isn't the same as knowing, tied back to `learning-craft/04`'s Feynman-technique framing). Session workflow (`CLAUDE.md`) now includes writing exercises and clicking through them before marking a unit done.
- `bun run check` clean throughout. `web/http-request-response-basics` remains the only `done` unit (1/218) — this session was entirely infrastructure for the exercise system, no new topic content.
- Next: pick the next unit and track together with the user — from here on, units should come with exercises unless there's a real reason not to.

## 2026-08-12 — Validated tables/charts in a real browser; fixed two contrast bugs

- The earlier "tables and charts work" claim had only been checked via `bun run build` succeeding and a raw HTML grep (`<table>`, `xychart-beta` present in output) — never actually looked at in a rendered page. User pushed back specifically because `applied-math/` will lean on this heavily. Built a throwaway fixture (GFM table, `xychart-beta` bar chart, `xychart-beta` line chart with 3 series approximating O(n)/O(n log n)/O(n²) growth curves, `pie` chart) and reviewed all four live in Chrome.
- GFM table: correct out of the box, no issues.
- `xychart-beta` bar and line charts: both rendered correctly (the 3-series line chart clearly showed the three growth curves as distinct, readable lines) but the plot area had its own mid-gray background rectangle that visibly mismatched the page's near-black `bg-slate-950` — `xyChart`'s background falls back to the Mermaid theme's `background` variable, which the built-in `dark`/`default` themes set to a shade lighter than our actual page background.
- `pie` chart: the default `dark` theme's slice palette (`pie1`..`pie6`) includes a near-black entry, which was effectively invisible against `bg-slate-950` — only distinguishable by its border.
- Fixed both in `Layout.astro`'s `mermaid.initialize()`: added `themeVariables` (background: transparent, brighter `pie1`-`pie6` palette with dark slice-label text for contrast, for both light/dark mode) and an explicit `xyChart: { backgroundColor: "transparent", plotColorPalette: ... }`. Deliberately layered these as _overrides on top of_ the existing `theme: "dark"/"default"` rather than switching to Mermaid's fully custom `"base"` theme, specifically to avoid risking a regression in the sequence-diagram appearance already fixed and validated earlier — re-checked the existing `web/http-request-response-basics` L2 sequence diagram after the change and confirmed no visual regression.
- Assessed `xychart-beta`'s real limits for `applied-math/`'s sake: bar/line only, no scatter, no true smooth curves (approximate with enough points), no dual-axis, no log scale. Judged sufficient for the large majority of illustration needs there; recommended against building custom charting infrastructure speculatively — revisit only if a specific `applied-math/` unit hits a real wall (needs scatter or log-scale) that an approximation can't cover. Documented in `CLAUDE.md`.
- Fixture removed after review. `bun run check` clean.
- Next: pick the next unit and track together with the user.

## 2026-08-12 — First systems/ unit: `systems/process` ("What actually happens when you run a program?")

- User chose `systems/process` as the next unit. Wrote all three levels: L1 (bullet summary of process/virtual memory/scheduler/segfault concepts), L2 (ASCII memory-layout diagram, a Mermaid `sequenceDiagram` for fork+exec, pseudocode, and the "why virtual memory enables isolation" explanation), L3 (four real, actually-run code examples: `pids.mjs` proving separate PIDs via `child_process.spawn`, `stack.mjs` proving the stack's finite size by overflowing it on purpose, `heap.mjs` proving heap growth via `process.memoryUsage()`, `maps.mjs` reading `/proc/self/maps` directly from the kernel to confirm the heap/stack regions are real — plus a failure-modes section distinguishing a caught `RangeError` from a real segfault).
- Added `exercises.json`: 3 quiz items (page-table isolation, why NULL derefs fault, why fork/exec are split) and 1 code exercise (`willStackOverflow(currentDepth, frameSizeBytes, stackLimitBytes)`, the same arithmetic the runtime does internally). Added `interactives.json`: a "recursion depth budget" demo (frame size × stack limit sliders → max safe recursion depth, charted across frame size).
- Found and fixed a real regression while reviewing L2 in the browser: reused `<br/>` inside Mermaid `Note over` text (one note with 2 lines, one with 3) to force line breaks — this renders garbled/overlapping text in this Mermaid setup, the same failure class already documented from message-text `<br/>` usage in the first unit, but not previously confirmed for Note text specifically, and the existing single-line-calibrated `.noteText` CSS transform doesn't correct it for multi-line notes. Fixed by rewriting both notes as single lines (matching the established message-text fix) rather than trying to extend the CSS fix to handle multi-line notes.

## 2026-08-12 — Fixed a real, site-wide Mermaid rendering bug found during browser verification

- Chrome bridge reconnected this session. First check (`people-management`'s flowchart) showed a tiny 16x16 broken-icon box instead of the diagram — initially misdiagnosed as caused by a literal `×` character in a node label (fixed that too, harmless either way), but the same tiny box reproduced on `career-craft` and `business-communication`'s flowcharts, which have no special characters at all — proved it was systemic, not a content bug.
- Root cause, confirmed by instrumenting Mermaid's render pipeline directly: `flowchart`/`xychart-beta` diagrams measure text via real DOM layout (`getBBox`), which returns 0 for anything inside a `display:none` ancestor. `LevelTabs.astro` hides the L2/L3 panels by default (`class="hidden"`), and `Layout.astro`'s mermaid script rendered every `.mermaid` block unconditionally at page load — so any flowchart/xychart living on a non-default tab collapsed to an empty box, permanently (Mermaid doesn't re-measure once `data-processed` is set). `sequenceDiagram`s (used in the two previously-verified units) measure text via canvas instead, which doesn't depend on visibility — that's the only reason this went undetected until now, across every unit written this session and, in hindsight, `business-communication`'s diagram too (never actually browser-checked before).
- Fixed in two files: `Layout.astro` now only calls `mermaid.run()` on diagrams that are currently visible (filtering out anything inside a `[data-tab-panel].hidden` ancestor) instead of rendering everything unconditionally on load; `LevelTabs.astro` dispatches a `mermaid:rerun` custom event after un-hiding a panel, which `Layout.astro` listens for and uses to render any diagram that was skipped, now that it's actually visible for the first time. Diagrams already rendered (`data-processed`) are never touched again, so no duplicate work on repeated tab switches.
- Verified via direct DOM inspection (`querySelector('svg').getAttribute('viewBox')` before/after clicking each unit's L2 tab) across all 15 written units with diagrams — every flowchart and xychart now reports a real, full-size viewBox instead of the `-8 -8 16 16` error box, and the two previously-working sequenceDiagrams (`systems/process`, `web/http-request-response-basics`) still render correctly (no regression). Screenshot capture itself was unreliable this session (CDP `Page.captureScreenshot` repeatedly timed out on this WSL/Vivaldi bridge, unrelated to the fix — page JS execution worked fine throughout), so verification relied on direct JS/DOM queries instead of visual screenshots.
- `bun run check` clean (lint, format, typecheck, content validation, tests, 34-page build).
- Takeaway for future sessions: a unit's diagram type determines whether hidden-tab rendering is even a risk — `sequenceDiagram` is safe, `flowchart`/`xychart-beta`/`pie` are not (all use DOM-based text measurement) — and browser-verifying only the _first_ unit of a new pattern isn't sufficient once tabs are hidden by default; this bug existed since `LevelTabs.astro` was built but was masked by every earlier verified unit happening to use `sequenceDiagram`.
- Next: continue advancing tracks with a second unit each, per the user's request to keep going.

## 2026-08-13 — Content-methodology pilot on `business-communication/audience-awareness` (not a new unit)

- User pushed back on the pedagogy across all 32 written units after actually using the site: L1/L2 explain before the reader feels a need to know (definitions/frameworks up front instead of a problem that creates the itch), no self-test moment before the answer is revealed, exercise banks too thin (~6/unit) for the 7/30/90-day spaced-repetition cycle to mean anything, and visual density (diagrams/tables/charts) too sparse. Agreed to fix the methodology + engine first, pilot it on **one already-written unit**, then stop for review before retrofitting the other 31 in future one-unit-per-session passes.
- **Engine changes** (not content): `ExercisePanel.astro` now shows only 2 randomly-picked pools per level per page view (was: all pools rendered at once), reshuffled on every load and on spaced-repetition reset — supports a much larger exercise bank without overwhelming the page. `ProgressToggle.astro` no longer gates on a static list of every pool id (`exerciseIds` prop, computed once at build time); it now reads whichever pools `ExercisePanel` is currently showing directly off the DOM (`hasExercises` boolean prop instead), so "Mark as done" only requires passing the currently-shown subset, not the full bank. `spaced-repetition.ts`'s `checkAndApplyReviewDue` now clears exercise state by localStorage key prefix instead of a passed-in id list, since there's no longer a fixed list to know ahead of time.
- **CLAUDE.md updated**: new rule 4b (problem-first, test-before-teach — L1 and every major L2 section must open with a concrete problem/prediction, not a definition, resolving it only afterward); exercises section rewritten to target ~40 items/unit (~13/13/14 across levels, pools of 2-3 variants) as a number being _piloted_, not a hard `validate:content` minimum, plus documentation of the new 2-shown/reshuffle/shown-subset-gating mechanic; visual-density guidance tightened from "at least one diagram" to "most units should carry 2-3 visual elements across L1-L3."
- **Pilot content**: rewrote `business-communication/audience-awareness`'s L1 and L2 to open with a concrete scenario/prediction before the framework (L3 already matched this voice — the payments-retry scenario — so left as-is); added one new table (mismatch-cost) and one new mermaid diagram (the four-question decision tree as a flowchart) to L2, on top of the existing flowchart+table. Expanded `exercises.json` from 6 to 40 items (13/13/14 across L1/L2/L3, quiz-only per rule 9 since this is a non-technical track), grouped into pools of 2-3 variants each (5-6 pools per level) so the new "2 of several" display actually has something to rotate through.
- `bun run check` clean (lint, format, typecheck, content validation, tests, 50-page build — unchanged unit/page count, this was a methodology pass not a new unit).
- **Browser-verified the full new mechanic end-to-end**, not just the build: confirmed each level tab shows exactly 2 of the unit's pools with a "Showing 2 of N — reload for a different pair" caption; answered exercises both right and wrong and confirmed the explanation always displays either way; confirmed "Mark as done" stayed locked while any shown pool was unanswered/failed, then unlocked once all _currently-shown_ pools (not all 40) were passed; clicked "Mark as done" and confirmed it persists across a reload; reloaded and confirmed a genuinely different 2-pool pair appeared per level (reshuffle-on-load works); confirmed both new L2 diagrams (flowchart + decision-tree) and both tables (lens table + new mismatch-cost table) render correctly, and the new problem-first L1/L2 openings read naturally in the browser, not just in the source.
- Next: **this needs user review before touching the other 31 units.** If the pilot's shape (voice, 40-exercise scale, 2-shown mechanic) is approved, the remaining 31 written units each need the same retrofit — one per session, per the existing one-unit-per-session rule — starting wherever the user wants to go next.

Wired into both `Scenario.astro` (automatic on every problem callout) and `ExercisePanel.astro` (automatic on every shown exercise) — no new authoring needed for existing or future units, documented in CLAUDE.md's new "Whiteboard" section.

## 2026-08-13 — Whiteboard refinement round (still `audience-awareness`, still not a new unit)

- User tested the whiteboard from the previous entry and gave three corrections:
  1. **"Chalks" — need selectable colors, not just black ink.** Added a 4-color swatch row per board style.
  2. **The reference panel was a literal duplicate of the visible callout/exercise — needed to be a distinct, structured, hierarchical recap instead.** Added a dedicated named slot on `Scenario.astro`, `<Fragment slot="facts">`, that a unit author writes separately from the flowing narrative (falls back to the narrative if unwritten, but new units should always write it — documented as a hard expectation in CLAUDE.md after shipping the duplicate version once). Wrote real `facts` content for both `audience-awareness` Scenario callouts (L1, L3).   3. **Two board presentations (white/green), and — caught during browser verification — colors need to remap between styles, not just persist unchanged.** Reworked the canvas to draw on a **transparent** layer with the board color as a separate CSS background (this also upgraded the eraser to a real `destination-out` erase instead of a same-color-as-background hack). White board uses marker colors (black/red/blue/green); green chalkboard uses chalk colors (white/yellow/pink/light-blue), paired by palette position. Switching board style now calls `recolorCanvas()` (nearest-RGB-match pixel remap against the previous palette, substituted with the new palette's positional counterpart) so a stroke keeps its _role_ (e.g. "the dark, primary color") rather than its literal RGB value, which was going near-invisible when carried unchanged onto the opposite-contrast board.
- `bun run check` clean after each round of changes. Verified the recolor logic isn't just visually plausible but exactly correct: drew a real stroke via dispatched pointer events, read back the exact RGB via `getImageData`, switched white → green → white, and confirmed the pixel value round-trips losslessly to the original.

## 2026-08-13 — Whiteboard: text tool + one more bug fix (still `audience-awareness`, still not a new unit)

- User caught a real bug from the previous round: switching board style was also changing the **reference panel's** background (it shouldn't — the facts recap is unrelated to the scratch-space presentation choice). Fixed by removing the coupling in `applyBoardStyle()`; only the canvas background changes now, verified via `getComputedStyle`-equivalent check before/after a style switch.

Committed text becomes ordinary raster pixels, so it persists/erases/recolors on board-style switch through the exact same mechanism as a pen stroke — no separate data model needed. Refactored the internal `erasing: boolean` into a proper `tool: "pen" | "eraser" | "text"` to support the three-way mode cleanly.
- Verified via dispatched pointer/keyboard events end-to-end: clicking Text spawns the input, typing + Enter removes it and commits, and the committed pixels were confirmed present in the exact clicked region via direct `getImageData` inspection (not just visually plausible — checked the actual opaque-pixel bounding box).

## 2026-08-13 — Two real bugs caught in the new L3 chart: invented data, and a site-wide dark-mode text color bug

- User caught the xychart's numbers (9/5/2, "Detail level 0-10") as invented — no real basis, not what the earlier data-grounding work in this session was supposed to prevent. Replaced with a genuinely countable metric: exact counts of concrete operational specifics named in each of the three quoted versions (rain/mechanism/magnitude/named-contact/time-of-day) — Groupmate 6, Teacher 3, Principal 1 — computed by literally listing and counting them against the actual quotes, with the count criteria spelled out in the surrounding text so a reader can verify it themselves. Also caught and corrected a wrong assumption baked into the original prose: word count does NOT track this pattern (the teacher version is actually the _longest_ at 72 words vs. the groupmate's 62) — said so explicitly instead of quietly using a metric that would have contradicted the point.
- User then caught a second, unrelated bug: the chart's title/axis text was dark-on-dark, unreadable. Root cause, found by reading mermaid's own source: `xyChart` title/axis colors fall back to `themeVariables.primaryTextColor`, which mermaid resolves from an internal `darkMode` boolean — **not** from the `theme: "dark"` string this project already passes to `mermaid.initialize()`. That boolean was never set, so `primaryTextColor` silently resolved to near-black regardless of site theme. Fixed in `src/layouts/Layout.astro`'s `chartThemeVariables` by setting `xyChart.titleColor`/`xAxisLabelColor`/`xAxisTitleColor`/`yAxisLabelColor`/`yAxisTitleColor`/tick/line colors explicitly for both light and dark branches, bypassing the broken fallback chain entirely.
- **This is a site-wide fix, not a pilot-only one** — `xychart-beta` is already used in 5 other written units (`applied-math/math-tool-prediction`, `applied-math/measurement-theory`, `software-design/cost-design`, `sustainable-performance/myth-linear-output`, `web/http-request-response-basics`), all of which had this same invisible-text bug and are now fixed by this one change, same pattern as the earlier-session hidden-tab mermaid collapse fix.
- `bun run check` clean. Verified the fix directly rather than trusting a screenshot (screenshots were unreliable this round, per the known WSL/Vivaldi CDP flakiness): read `getComputedStyle(...).fill` on the actual rendered title and axis-label `<text>` elements and confirmed they resolve to the exact hex values set in the fix (`#e2e8f0` title, `#cbd5e1` axis labels), not just "looks lighter."

Implemented:
  - `global.css`: new one-shot keyframe animations — `fade-slide-in` (reveals), `shake` (wrong answer), `pop` (correct answer / "Mark as done"), `fill-bar` (a bar's `width` animating from 0 to a `--target-width` custom property set inline).
  - `ExercisePanel.astro`: shown pools now get `animate-fade-slide-in` with a staggered `animation-delay` when picked; quiz/code feedback gets `animate-pop` (pass) or `animate-shake` (fail); the explanation/solution reveal also gets `animate-fade-slide-in`.
  - `LevelTabs.astro`: switching tabs now applies `animate-fade-slide-in` to the incoming panel (remove-then-reflow-then-add, so repeated clicks re-trigger it) instead of an instant cut.
  - `ProgressToggle.astro`: a successful "Mark as done" click applies `animate-pop` to the button (same reflow re-trigger technique).

This was the answer to "let me change the problem's values and see the result update in real time" — no new engine work needed, since `InteractiveDemo`/`interactives.json` already existed and was simply unused by this pilot.
  - **A real bug surfaced and fixed during this round, not just cosmetic**: the first implementation used a JS `requestAnimationFrame` double-class-swap (set the "from" state, then flip to the "to" state next frame) for the pool-reveal and tab-fade animations. This got stuck permanently at the "from" state (opacity: 0) during browser verification — traced to `document.visibilityState === "hidden"` on the automation tab, which suspends `requestAnimationFrame` callbacks entirely. Rather than special-case the test environment, switched both to a declarative CSS `animation` (keyframes) applied via a simple `classList.add`, which has no rAF/foreground dependency — confirmed correct by forcing the Web Animations API `.finish()` and checking the resulting computed style lands exactly on the intended end state (opacity 1, translateY 0, bar width exactly 68% of its parent).
- `bun run check` clean. Browser-verified via direct DOM/Web-Animations-API inspection rather than screenshots (this session's tab was backgrounded per `document.visibilityState`, making real-time screenshot/animation-timing checks unreliable — same class of flakiness noted in earlier sessions): confirmed `animate-shake`/`animate-pop` apply correctly on wrong/right answers with radios locked afterward, the L1→L2 tab switch re-renders Mermaid diagrams on the freshly-shown panel with real (non-collapsed) viewBoxes, the whiteboard bar's forced-finished width is exactly 68% of its container, the slider's live output at 0/50/70% is $680/$0, $340/$160, $204/$296 (matching the back-derived formula and the unit's actual story), and clicking "Mark as done" (after passing all shown exercises across all three levels) applies `animate-pop` and flips to "Done (review in 7d)".

## 2026-08-13 — Three global UI bugs found by the user testing the live preview, all fixed at the engine level

- User tested the running preview themselves (both retrofitted units) and reported three cross-cutting issues, none specific to one unit:
  1. **Quiz radio buttons didn't clearly show which choice was selected** before grading — the native radio dot alone was too subtle to scan at a glance. Fixed in `ExercisePanel.astro`'s `renderQuiz`: each choice's `<label>` now gets a visible border + background + bold text the moment its radio is checked (a `change` listener toggles between a defined "selected" and "unselected" label class across the group), on top of the native dot, not instead of it — pre-grading only; the existing post-grading correct/wrong color-coding is unchanged.
  2. **Mermaid `xychart-beta` bar charts cut off long x-axis category labels** (e.g. "SHA-256 (GPU cluster)") — mermaid's default `xAxis.labelRotation` is `0` (horizontal) with no wrapping, so labels wider than their allotted per-category slot overlapped/were clipped. Fixed in `Layout.astro`'s `mermaid.initialize()` by adding a top-level `xyChart: { xAxis: { labelRotation: -30, labelFontSize: 11 } }` config (separate from the existing `themeVariables.xyChart` color overrides, which only handle theming, not layout) — angling labels gives each one far more effective width for the same horizontal footprint, the standard fix for this class of chart. Site-wide, not pilot-only: every `xychart-beta` bar chart across all units benefits automatically.
  3. **`InteractiveDemo.astro`'s own SVG line chart clipped numbers/labels at certain slider positions** — the marker dot's value label was always drawn to the dot's right and slightly above it, and the y-axis min/max labels used a fixed 44px left margin; both assumptions broke once a real relationship (this session's crack-time demo) produced long compact-notation labels (`754.6 B years`) or a marker near the chart's right edge. Fixed in `InteractiveDemo.astro`'s `buildChart()`:
     - The left/right chart margins are now computed per-render from the actual current y-axis and x-axis-max label lengths, growing beyond the old fixed minimums instead of clipping against them.
     - The marker's value label flips to the dot's _left_ side (with matching `text-anchor`) whenever there isn't room on the right, and its vertical position is clamped to stay inside the plot area — so a label can no longer run past the SVG's own bounds and get silently hidden by overflow, at any slider position.
  - All three fixes are general engine/layout fixes, not specific to either retrofitted unit — same category as the earlier currency-prefix and compact-number-formatting fixes from prior sessions.
- Verified directly in the browser rather than trusting the build: checked/unchecked radio label classes toggle correctly; read the actual rendered `<text>` geometry (`getBoundingClientRect()` compared against the SVG's own bounding rect, not `getBBox()` — which ignores the rotation transform and gave false positives on the first check) for both the L3 bar chart (confirms −30° rotation, no clipping, no label overlap) and the L2 interactive demo at all four slider corners (min/min, min/max, max/min, max/max) — zero overflowing text nodes in every case. Also re-checked the original pilot's demo (`business-communication/audience-awareness`) at both slider extremes to confirm no regression from the shared-component changes.
- `bun run check`-equivalent run in full — clean, same pre-existing unrelated warnings as before.

## 2026-08-13 — Mermaid bar-chart labels still clipping after the first fix; root-caused to a mermaid internal estimation bug

- User pasted a screenshot from testing the live preview themselves showing the L3 bar chart's rotated x-axis labels still visibly cut off ("SHA-256 (GPU cluster)" missing its "SHA-" prefix, etc.), despite the −30° rotation fix from the previous entry. The earlier verification (`getBoundingClientRect()` on text vs. the SVG's own rect) had been insufficient — it doesn't account for content that overflows the SVG's _internal_ `viewBox` coordinate space specifically (browsers default the root `<svg>` element to `overflow: hidden`, confirmed via `getComputedStyle`), which is a different, stricter boundary than the SVG's rendered client rect.
- Root-caused properly this time by transforming each `<text>`'s `getBBox()` through its actual `getCTM()` into the chart's own viewBox coordinates and comparing against `[0, 0, 700, 500]` directly. Found the real problem: labels were overflowing the chart's **bottom** edge (up to ~18px), not the sides — the −30° rotation fix from the previous entry had actually fully solved the horizontal/side clipping (confirmed zero left/right overflow at every step below), but rotating labels adds vertical footprint that mermaid's default `height: 500` doesn't budget for.
- Tried increasing `xyChart.height` (500 → 580 → 650) first — this barely moved the overflow. Reading mermaid's own `xychartDiagram` source clarified why: the x-axis's reserved space is computed from its own estimated footprint (`sin(rotation)×textWidth + cos(rotation)×textHeight + labelPadding×2`), not as a percentage of total chart height — so growing `height` mostly just grows the plot/bar area, not the axis label area. The real fix was `xAxis.labelPadding` (default `5`, feeds directly into that reserved-space formula) — raised to `20`, which closed most of the gap, then to `28`, which reached zero overflow on every axis label in the live chart. The remaining gap at each step was consistent with mermaid's own text-measurement estimate slightly under-shooting the real rendered bbox for rotated text (an internal mermaid quirk, not something wrong with this project's config) — `labelPadding` buys back that margin directly rather than fighting the exact trig estimate.
- Reverted the unhelpful `height: 650` override; the final `Layout.astro` mermaid config is just `xyChart: { xAxis: { labelRotation: -30, labelFontSize: 11, labelPadding: 28 } }`.
- Verified with the corrected getCTM-based check: zero overflowing text nodes (any edge) on `security/authentication-fundamentals` L3's bar chart, and re-checked it doesn't regress the other three existing `xychart-beta` charts (`web/http-request-response-basics`, `applied-math/math-tool-prediction`) — zero overflow there too, confirming the fix generalizes rather than being tuned to one chart's specific label lengths.
- `bun run check`-equivalent run in full — clean, same pre-existing unrelated warnings.
- **Lesson for future verification**: `getBoundingClientRect()` comparisons against a parent's client rect are not sufficient to catch SVG-internal clipping — the reliable check is transforming `getBBox()` through the element's own `getCTM()` into the root SVG's viewBox coordinate space and comparing there, since that's the actual boundary the browser's default `overflow: hidden` on `<svg>` enforces.
- Next: unchanged — awaiting the user's opinion on the second pilot (now with all three reported UI bugs fixed) before picking a third track.

## 2026-08-13/14 — Agent-orchestrated retrofit of remaining units to the new content bar, batch 1 (3 units)

- User asked to dispatch agents to retrofit all already-written units (30 remaining) to the validated visual/interactive/animation content bar, one unit each, until done. Used the `Agent` tool with `isolation: "worktree"` (parallel git worktrees under `.claude/worktrees/agent-<id>/`, each on its own branch) so agents don't collide on file writes, batching 3 at a time.
- **Process bug found and fixed**: none of the pilot work (both retrofitted units, the shared component/CSS/lib changes) had been committed yet — it only existed as uncommitted working-tree changes. Worktree agents branch from committed history, so they were invisible to it. First batch of 3 agents all started from a stale base missing the whole pilot infrastructure; one correctly detected this and stopped (git-teamwork/checkout, first attempt), one proceeded against the stale ruleset (applied-math/measurement-theory, first attempt — discarded), one worked around it by manually copying in the missing shared files (learning-craft/exposure-understanding — completed successfully, but its branch carried a hand-duplicated copy of shared infra alongside its real content). Fixed at the root by committing the full pilot work (`1ced061`) so every subsequent/relaunched worktree branches from the correct, complete base. Also fixed two things this surfaced: `.gitignore` was missing `.claude/` (agent worktree scratch space), and `eslint.config.mjs`'s `ignores` didn't exclude `.claude/**`, so ESLint's TS parser choked on multiple nested `tsconfig.json`s once worktrees existed.

## 2026-08-14 — Retrofit batch 2 (3 units) — found and worked around a session-wide worktree-isolation bug

- Launched 3 more worktree-isolated agents (`applied-math/math-tool-prediction`, `business-communication/status-updates-build-trust`, `career-craft/asking-feedback`) after committing batch 1 (`6d93976`), expecting them to branch from that commit.

Committing more work to `main` afterward did not change what base new `isolation: "worktree"` agents get — the base appears to be fixed/cached per-session from the first use, not re-read from current `HEAD` on each launch. This is a session-scoped environment quirk, not something fixable by committing more or waiting.
- Two agents (`math-tool-prediction`, `asking-feedback`) discovered this only after writing full retrofit content (using `<Scenario>` and `reference`/`learnMore` exercise fields that don't exist at that stale base) and hitting a `bun run build` failure on the unresolved `Scenario.astro` import — correctly stopped rather than reimplementing the missing shared infrastructure themselves. Verified directly afterward: `git status`/`git diff` on the main checkout showed clean, zero divergence from `HEAD` — no actual damage, but flagging the incident since it's a real risk pattern (an agent operating outside its assigned worktree) worth watching for in future batches.
- Recovered the same way as batch 1's `exposure-understanding` case: since the two content-complete agents had only ever touched their own unit's `src/content/<track>/<unit>/*` files (never the shared infra they were missing — they couldn't, it didn't exist in their worktree), their content was extracted directly and copied into the main checkout, where `Scenario.astro` etc. already exist. No rebase needed. Ran the full guardrail suite fresh (`lint`, `format`, `typecheck`, `validate:content`, `test`, `generate:roadmap`, `build`) against the merged result in `main` — all clean.
- Browser-verified both units this time (extension was connected): L1/L2/L3 render, Scenario callouts with visual `facts` panels render correctly, exercise pools show/gate correctly (`data-shown-ids`/`data-ready`), and both units' interactive-demo sliders were dragged to every param's min/max and checked via the `getBBox()`-through-`getCTM()`-into-viewBox geometry check — zero overflowing text in any case, on both units' demos.

Resumed the session after the reset per the user's message ("ya puedes continuar ya son las 2").
- Before assuming the work was lost, inspected each worktree directly: all three had, in fact, already finished writing full content (L1/L3 `.mdx`, L2, 38-40 item `exercises.json`, `interactives.json`) before being cut off — the agents' last visible message ("now let's build exercises.json...") undersold how far they'd actually gotten; verified this by parsing each JSON file directly (`python3 -m json.tool`-equivalent) rather than trusting the transcript.

- **Found and fixed two real content bugs during the fresh guardrail run** (neither agent could have caught these — both failures only surface once merged onto the correct base, which none of them had access to):
  1. `validate:content` caught a genuine authoring bug in `git-teamwork/snapshots-manual-copies/exercises.json`: two variants sharing `poolId: "content-dedup"` had different `reference` HTML — the schema requires identical `reference`/`learnMore` across all variants of a pool (only one variant renders per view, so they must agree). Fixed by copying the canonical variant's `reference`/`learnMore` onto the other.
  2. `bun run build` failed rendering `/infra-delivery/docker` with `Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'length')` inside `InteractiveDemo.astro`. Root cause: the `docker` agent had manually added `import InteractiveDemo ...` and `<InteractiveDemo id="build-time-vs-changed-layers" />` directly inside `L2-concept.mdx`'s prose — but `InteractiveDemo.astro` expects a `demos` array prop (plural, a list of full demo objects) and is meant to be rendered exactly once, automatically, by `src/pages/[track]/[unit]/index.astro` per level — not manually invoked from content with an `id` string, which the component has no code path for at all. No other unit does this. Fixed by removing the stray import and the manual `<InteractiveDemo>` tag from the MDX; the demo still renders correctly since the page template already handles it. Worth flagging in future agent prompts: interactive demos are automatic, never manually embedded in MDX.
  3. `git-teamwork/snapshots-manual-copies` kept `L2-concept.md` as `.md` (no `<Scenario>` needed there), consistent with the `checkout` unit's pattern — correctly followed the established convention rather than converting every level to `.mdx` by default.
- After both fixes, full guardrail suite clean (`lint`, `format`, `typecheck`, `validate:content`, `test`, `generate:roadmap`, `build`), and all three units browser-verified: L1/L2/L3 render, mermaid/xychart-beta visuals correct, exercise gating wired (`shownIds` populated correctly on all three), and every interactive-demo slider dragged to min/max on every param with zero SVG text overflow via the `getBBox()`/`getCTM()` geometry check.
- 11 of 30 remaining units done (adds `white`, `snapshots-manual-copies`, `docker`). 19 remain. Lesson for future batches: always run the full guardrail suite fresh in the main checkout after merging, even when every agent self-reported clean results — both bugs this round were invisible to the agents because they never had a working base to actually build/validate against.

## 2026-08-17 — New unit: web/css-cascade-specificity, first run of `/next-unit`

- Added a `.claude/commands/next-unit.md` slash command that codifies CLAUDE.md's full session workflow (pick unit → scope check → scaffold → write content → update ROADMAP.md → `bun run check` → browser-verify → update PROGRESS.md → suggest next), so future sessions can start with one command instead of re-deriving the process each time.
- Immediately used it to write the first genuinely new unit since the retrofit project finished (as opposed to retrofitting an already-`done` unit). Since the user didn't name a track, the command's tie-break logic picked the tracks with the lowest done/total ratio (`web` and `systems`, both 2/17) and offered 3 candidates via `AskUserQuestion`; the user picked `web/css-cascade-specificity` — "How do we style once and reuse everywhere? (CSS cascade & specificity)".
- Wrote all three levels directly (not via a subagent, since this was a single focused unit): L1 opens on a rule that "should have worked" but didn't (a later `.btn-primary` losing to an older, more specific `#page .toolbar .btn`); L2 covers the full 4-step cascade chain, specificity as a componentwise tuple (not additive), why utility/BEM codebases stay flat while id-nested legacy codebases develop "specificity wars," and the two modern escape valves (`:where()`, `@layer`); L3 is a full incident (a design-system migration silently failing on old pages) with a real, runnable specificity-calculator function used to diagnose it, plus the `:where()`/`@layer` fixes and an extension question about third-party CSS you don't control.
- Caught and fixed one real specificity-direction bug while drafting L1's comparison table (`button.btn` vs `.btn-primary` — the tie-break on the element column was initially stated backwards) before it shipped, by hand-tracing the tuple comparison rule.
- Wrote 40 exercises (13/13/14, 18 pools) and self-verified all 38 code-exercise test assertions actually pass their solutions by extracting and running them directly out of the committed JSON (not just a hand-written reimplementation) — this caught 2 pools where `reference` differed by variant (only `learnMore` is required to match by CLAUDE.md's rule, but `reference` must too), fixed before merge. Also added `interactives.json` — a slider on DOM nesting depth showing legacy id-based specificity growing linearly while flat utility/BEM specificity stays constant at 1, deliberately using raw class counts instead of the id=100/class=10 additive shorthand so the demo can't accidentally imply "enough classes eventually beat an id," which L2 explicitly warns is false.
- Ran the full guardrail suite (`generate:roadmap`, `lint`, `format`, `typecheck`, `validate:content`, `test`, `build`) — clean. Browser-verified: exercise pools populated on all 3 levels, a quiz answered wrong (radios lock, explanation shows), an L3 code exercise run unmodified (solution/explanation reveal on failure too), and the interactive demo dragged min→max confirming the legacy-nesting output tracks the slider 1:1 while the flat-methodology output stays at 1.
- Flipped `web/css-cascade-specificity` to `done` in `ROADMAP.md`. 33 total `done` units now (2 pilots + 30 retrofitted + 1 new).
- Next: any track/unit — no particular one suggested, since this session used the new tie-break logic (`web`/`systems` still tied at 2/17 done, followed by `git-teamwork` at 2/16).

## 2026-08-17 — New unit: systems/algorithmic-complexity, first autonomous-loop unit

- User asked to "leave it running" — confirmed via two quick questions that the loop should (a) auto-pick the next unit each time without pausing to confirm, and (b) commit each finished unit locally (no push) as it completes, push handled separately by the user. This entry is the first unit written entirely under that mode.
- Auto-picked `systems/algorithmic-complexity` (tied with `web` at 2/17 done, `systems` selected this round) — "Why is my code fast on my machine but slow in production? (algorithmic complexity, Big-O in practice)".
- L1 opens on a "remove duplicate customer IDs" function that passes CI on a 200-row fixture and times out on a 500,000-row production import; L2 traces the incident's pseudocode line by line (`record.id not in result` hiding an O(n) scan inside what looks like a single check), covers the standard Big-O simplification rules (drop constants, drop lower-order terms, nested-vs-sequential), and the worst-case/average-case distinction for hash lookups; L3 reproduces the incident with real, runnable JS and an actual Node benchmark.
- Caught a real fabrication risk before it shipped: the L3 benchmark originally had invented "typical output" numbers for the array-vs-Set comparison. Actually ran the benchmark code in Node instead of asserting fictional timings — the real numbers were noisier than a clean textbook example (JIT warm-up and small-n measurement noise overshot the O(n²) prediction on the first step, undershot on the second), so the content was rewritten to show the ACTUAL measured numbers and explicitly teach why a single small-n timing isn't proof of anything — a more honest and arguably more useful lesson than a fabricated clean ~16x-every-step number would have been.

## 2026-08-17 — New unit: logic/problem-decomposition

L3 reproduces the exact numbers in verified code: `decomposeTiming`, `identifyBottleneck`, `isExhaustiveDecomposition`, and `hasOverlap` (the last two directly encoding the two failure-mode checks as executable, testable logic), plus an extension on non-additive decompositions (onboarding confusion, where pieces need to be defined before they're even measurable).

## 2026-08-17 — New unit: systems/sockets

- Auto-picked `systems/sockets` — a 2-way tie at the lowest done/total ratio (`systems`, `web`, both 3/17), broken alphabetically — "How do two programs talk to each other?".
- Technical track. Built around one incident: a chat server that assumes one `data` event equals one complete JSON message — reliably true on localhost, but false once a real network splits a longer message across two `data` events, producing a downstream parse failure with no crash at the socket layer itself.
- L1 introduces ports (program-level addressing beneath the machine-level IP address), states TCP's core guarantee precisely (ordered, reliable bytes — not messages), and frames the practical skill as verifying the one-event-one-message assumption rather than trusting it. L2 gives a mermaid sequence diagram of the TCP three-way handshake (contrasted with UDP's lack of one), two concrete framing schemes (newline-delimited vs. length-prefixed) with their tradeoffs, and explains precisely why localhost testing systematically avoids the conditions that trigger this bug. L3 reproduces the exact incident in verified code (`naiveHandler` showing the bug, `createLineFramer` as the fix — buffering across `data` events with a `while` loop specifically to handle both split messages and multiple-messages-in-one-chunk), plus an extension on when length-prefixed framing is the more robust choice over newline-delimited.
- All L3 code was verified twice: standalone in Node before writing (`/tmp/verify_sockets.mjs`), and again by extracting and running every code-exercise `solution`+`tests` pair directly from the committed `exercises.json` — all 14 code items pass, including edge cases (empty chunks, empty messages between consecutive delimiters, independent framer instances not sharing buffer state).
- Wrote 40 exercises (13/13/14, 15 pools) — quiz for L1/L2, code for L3 using `naiveHandler` and `createLineFramer` (basic, multi-message, and edge-case variants).
- Built `interactives.json`: an explicitly-labeled illustrative model (not a precise network simulation) of how message size relates to the minimum number of TCP-buffer-sized fragments a message could split across — defaulting to 1000 bytes (1 fragment) and showing the count grow as size increases toward the conditions that actually triggered the incident.
- **Caught a real MDX build failure during the previous unit's session and applied the lesson here**: avoided backslash-escaped quotes in JSX attribute strings this time; used a template-literal-style JSX expression (`{"{\"...\"}"}`) for embedding a JSON snippet with quotes inside a `Fragment` body instead, which is valid MDX/JSX. Build passed clean on the first attempt.
- Ran the full guardrail suite (`generate:roadmap`, `lint`, `format`, `typecheck`, `validate:content`, `test`, `build`) — clean, no errors. Removed the stale scaffolded `L1-summary.md`/`L3-deep-dive.md` stubs.

## 2026-08-18 — New unit: git-teamwork/merge

- Auto-picked `git-teamwork/merge` — `git-teamwork` was the clear lowest done/total ratio (3/16) — "How do two people edit the same file without destroying each other's work? (merge, conflicts)".
- Technical track. Built around one scenario: Alex and Sam branch off the same `cart.js` commit — Alex adds a null-check guard, Sam changes the `TAX_RATE` constant. When their lines don't overlap, git auto-merges silently; when both independently edit the exact same line to different values, it produces a real conflict — and when they land on the _same_ new value independently, it still doesn't conflict.
- L1 introduces merge base, three-way merge, auto-merge vs. conflict, conflict markers, and fast-forward as key terms tied to the scenario. L2 gives a mermaid flowchart of the three-way comparison (base→ours, base→theirs→merged), a pseudocode decision procedure per base line, a table contrasting auto-merge/conflict/fast-forward outcomes, and the mechanical (not superstitious) reason short-lived branches reduce conflict risk. ## 2026-08-18 — New unit: applied-math/asymptotic-analysis

- Auto-picked `applied-math/asymptotic-analysis` — a 2-way tie at the lowest done/total ratio (`applied-math`, `people-management`, both 3/15), broken alphabetically — "How do I reason precisely about growth as input size increases? (asymptotic analysis, Big-O put on rigorous footing)".
- Technical track. Built around one scenario: a candidate benchmarks their algorithm at n = 5 and n = 10, sees the runtime roughly double, and ships it confident it's linear. In production n reaches 100,000+ and the service falls over — the real function was `T(n) = 3n² + 100n`, whose `100n` term still dominated the `3n²` term at the small sizes tested, but whose `3n²` term takes over completely at scale.
- L1 introduces growth rate, dominant term, and the formal O/Ω/Θ vocabulary tied to the scenario. L2 gives the precise formal definitions (`T(n) ≤ c·f(n)` for `n ≥ n₀`), a table contrasting O/Ω/Θ, a mermaid `xychart-beta` showing the crossover point (n ≈ 33) where `3n²` overtakes `100n`, and a table of the doubling ratio drifting from ≈2.3 (n=5→10) to ≈4.0 (n=100,000→200,000) for the exact same function. L3 builds a verified operation-counting toolkit — `countLinearScan`, `countNestedLoop`, `countMergeSortComparisons` (a real recursive merge sort), `estimateGrowthExponent` (doubling-ratio → log2 exponent estimate), `classifyGrowth` — and reproduces the misleading benchmark with real numbers, plus an extension comparing `3n² + 100n` against `3n² − 100n` to show the direction of small-n bias isn't fixed.
- All L3 code was verified twice: standalone in `/tmp/verify_asymp.mjs` before writing, and again by extracting and running every code-exercise `solution`+`tests` pair directly from the committed `exercises.json` — all 22 test assertions across 14 code items pass. Caught and fixed two real bugs during exercise verification: a `toBe(2)` floating-point-equality test that failed at 1.9997... (fixed to a tolerance check), and a pool variant whose test referenced `estimateGrowthExponent` without that helper being part of its own `solution`/`starterCode` (fixed by giving that variant its own combined solution).
- Wrote 40 exercises (13/13/14, 19 pools) — quiz for L1/L2 (including a 3-variant pool on how a subtracted lower-order term biases small-n measurements in the opposite direction from an added one), code for L3 using the verified functions above.
- Built `interactives.json`: a `doubling-ratio-explorer` demo (level 2) — dragging the measurement point n from 5 to 100,000 shows the doubling ratio for `3n² + 100n` climbing live from 2.26 toward its true asymptotic value of 4.
- Ran the full guardrail suite (`generate:roadmap`, `lint`, `format`, `typecheck` — 0 errors/0 warnings/69 pre-existing hints, `validate:content` — 51 units checked, `test` — 9/9 pass, `build` — 69 pages) — clean.
- Chrome extension bridge connected — full browser verification via `astro preview --background`: 3 exercise panels populated with `shownIds`, 1 mermaid diagram, 1 interactive demo; dragged the interactive demo's slider to 100,000 and confirmed the ratio updated live to 4; ran an L1 quiz with a wrong answer (confirmed "Not quite" + explanation), reloaded and answered a freshly-shuffled pool correctly (confirmed "Correct" + explanation); ran the `classifyGrowth` code exercise with a deliberately broken implementation (always returns the same string — confirmed both tests failed with red markers), then the correct implementation (confirmed both tests passed with emerald markers) — verified end-to-end through the actual sandboxed Worker execution path.

## 2026-08-18 — New unit: git-teamwork/merge-rebase

- Auto-picked `git-teamwork/merge-rebase` (5/18 done in `git-teamwork/`, tied-lowest ratio) — "Why does `git pull` sometimes create a mess? (merge vs. rebase, fast-forward)".
- Technical track. Built around one running example across all three levels: an engineer's clean Monday `git pull` versus Tuesday's identical command adding an unexplained "Merge branch 'main' of ..." commit, because local made one commit of its own before pulling on Tuesday.
- L1 introduces fast-forward, merge commit, rebase, and divergence as key terms. ## 2026-08-19 — New unit: systems/race-conditions

- Auto-picked `systems/race-conditions` — tied-lowest done/total ratio (5/17) alongside `web`, alphabetical tiebreak — "How do we avoid two processes corrupting shared data? (race conditions, locks, atomicity)".
- Technical track. Built around one running example across all three levels: a flash-sale item with 1 unit of stock, where two concurrent purchase requests both read `stock = 1` and both succeed, oversold by 1 — the classic time-of-check-to-time-of-use (TOCTOU) bug.
- L1 introduces race condition, critical section, atomicity, and lock (mutex) as key terms. L2 gives a mermaid sequence diagram of the exact interleaving that causes the bug, a make-it-atomic-vs-use-a-lock comparison table, and a section on why adding more checks or delays doesn't close the gap, only relocates it. L3 reproduces the bug with real, runnable Node.js code (`InventoryStore` with simulated DB round-trip delay via `await delay(5)`), verified to actually reproduce 2 successes against 1 unit of stock, then fixes it two ways: a synchronous `purchaseAtomic` (no `await` in the critical section, verified 1 success), and a hand-rolled `Mutex` class with `purchaseLocked` (verified 1 success out of 3 concurrent buyers), plus an extend-it question confirming the atomic fix holds with 3 buyers too (verified 1 success, matching the locked version).
- Wrote 40 exercises (13/13/14, 19 pools) — L1/L2 quiz-only, L3 mixing 4 code items (2 pools: `purchase-atomic`, `mutex`) with 10 quiz items; all 4 code exercises' solutions independently verified against their tests via a standalone Node script before writing, using a hand-written `expect()` shim matching the site's actual grading API.
- Built `interactives.json`: an `oversold-units-by-concurrent-buyers` demo (level 3) charting `unitsOversold = concurrentBuyers - 1`, back-derived from the exact racy code and verified via a Node script across buyer counts 2–20 (deterministic, since all buyers hit the same fixed delay window) — reproduces L3's actual 2-buyer result (1 oversold) at the default.
- **Caught and fixed a real Mermaid rendering bug during browser verification**: L2's sequence-diagram flowchart's first node, `A["Someone asks a direct question"]` (from the corporate-politics unit earlier this session) doesn't apply here, but a near-identical class of bug did: a manual markdown line-wrap split the word "time-of-check" across two source lines as `time-of-\ncheck`, which Markdown's soft-line-break-to-space rule rendered as the literal typo "time-of- check" in prose. Fixed by removing the mid-word line break; confirmed no other hyphenated word had the same issue via `grep`.
- **Found and fixed a real, previously-undiscovered bug in the shared `ExercisePanel.astro` component, affecting every code exercise site-wide, not just this unit's content**: the sandboxed Web Worker ran `eval(code)` once to define the starter/solution code, then ran each test's `eval(t.expr)` as a _separate_ eval call. `class` declarations (unlike `function` declarations) created via direct `eval()` are scoped to that eval call itself in JavaScript and don't leak into the enclosing function scope — confirmed via a standalone Node repro (`class Foo {...}` via eval, then `new Foo()` in a second eval throws `Foo is not defined`, while an equivalent `function Bar(){}` works fine). This meant any code exercise using `class` syntax in its starter code (this unit's two `mutex-pool` exercises) would fail every test with a spurious "X is not defined" instead of a real assertion result, regardless of what the learner wrote. ## 2026-08-19 — New unit: security/symmetric-asymmetric-encryption-basics

- Auto-picked `security/symmetric-asymmetric-encryption-basics` — lowest done/total ratio (4/13) — "How do we keep data unreadable to anyone but the intended recipient? (symmetric vs. asymmetric encryption basics)".
- Technical track. Built around one running example across all three levels: Alice needs to send Bob a confidential contract by email — she encrypts it with a symmetric key, then has to email Bob that same key over the same interceptable channel, which is the key distribution problem asymmetric encryption exists to solve.
- L1 introduces symmetric encryption, asymmetric encryption, public/private key pair, and key distribution problem as key terms. L2 gives two mermaid sequence diagrams (symmetric: both file and key cross the network; asymmetric: only the public key does), a cost comparison table, and a section on why real systems combine both as hybrid encryption (previewing the next unit, TLS/HTTPS). L3 implements and runs real Node `crypto`-module code: AES-256-GCM symmetric encrypt/decrypt (verified correct-key recovery and wrong-key authentication failure), RSA-2048 asymmetric encrypt/decrypt, a timing comparison measuring the actual per-operation cost (symmetric ~0.028ms vs. asymmetric private-key ~0.55ms on an identical 32-byte payload — about 19.5x slower, independently verified via a standalone Node script before writing), a full hybrid-encryption roundtrip (AES for the message, RSA-wrapped AES key, 256-byte wrapped key confirmed independent of message length), and an extend-it question on what a 100x-longer message would change (only the symmetric step's cost, not the asymmetric wrapping cost).
- Wrote 40 exercises (13/13/14, 19 pools) — L1/L2 quiz-only, L3 mixing 4 code items with 10 quiz items. The 4 code exercises use a simplified XOR-cipher illustration of the symmetric round-trip property (explicitly labeled in-exercise as a toy illustration, not real cryptography — L3's actual encryption uses real AES-256-GCM/RSA-2048 via Node's crypto module) plus a `chooseEncryptionApproach`/`wrappedKeySizeBytes` pair modeling the real hybrid-encryption size-limit logic from L3. This choice was necessary because the exercise sandbox is a plain browser Web Worker with no Node `crypto` module and no synchronous Web Crypto API, so genuine AES/RSA operations aren't testable there — all 4 solutions independently verified against their exact tests via a standalone Node script before writing.
- Built `interactives.json`: a `symmetric-vs-asymmetric-decrypt-time` demo (level 3) charting `symmetricTimeMs`/`asymmetricTimeMs` as linear functions of operation count, using the exact measured per-operation rates from L3 — reproduces L3's actual 500-operation result (14.2ms vs. 277.3ms) exactly at the default. Verified in the browser.
- Ran the full guardrail suite (`generate:roadmap` — 218 units/16 tracks, `lint` — 0 issues, `format` — clean, `typecheck` — 0 errors/0 warnings/69 pre-existing hints, `validate:content` — 76 units checked, `test` — 9/9 pass, `build` — 94 pages) — clean.

## 2026-08-19 — New unit: applied-math/probability-distributions-uniform

- Auto-picked `applied-math/probability-distributions-uniform` — tied for lowest done/total ratio (5/15, 33.3%) with `infra-delivery`, `logic`, and `people-management`, winning the alphabetical tiebreak — "How do I model something that involves chance? (probability distributions — uniform, binomial, normal — and where each shows up in systems)".
- Technical track. Built around three tied real-code scenarios across all three levels rather than one running example, since the unit covers three distinct distributions: (1) a load balancer picking 1 of 4 servers uniformly at random, where server 3 got 64 of 200 requests (32% vs. expected 25%) — is that a bug? (2) a downstream dependency with a 2% independent failure rate, and what failure count in 500 requests should actually page someone; (3) end-to-end latency as the sum of 6 independent uniformly-distributed hop delays, demonstrating the Central Limit Theorem.
- L1 introduces probability distribution, uniform distribution, binomial distribution, normal distribution/CLT, and standard deviation as key terms. L2 gives an `xychart-beta` bar chart of the uniform distribution's flat shape, a binomial formulas table (mean/variance/stddev) plus an `xychart-beta` histogram of the 500-request/2%-failure-rate distribution (bucket probabilities independently computed, not guessed — an earlier draft's made-up bucket values were caught and replaced with verified ones), a mermaid flowchart of the CLT (6 flat delays summing to a bell shape), and a picking-the-right-tool comparison table. ## 2026-08-19 — New unit: web/cors

- Auto-picked `web/cors` — tied for lowest done/total ratio (6/17, 35.3%) with `business-communication` and `corporate-politics`, winning the alphabetical tiebreak — "Why does calling an API from the browser fail unpredictably? (CORS, async/await, error handling)".
- Technical track. Built around one running example across all three levels: `fetch('https://api.example.com/users')` from `https://app.example.com` works fine in Postman but fails in the browser with no data reaching the app, even though the API's own server logs show the request was handled successfully — because CORS is enforced entirely browser-side, after the server already responded. A second, deliberately separate thread covers the classic async/await gotcha: `fetch()` only rejects on network-level failures, not on HTTP error statuses like 404/500.
- L1 introduces same-origin policy, CORS, preflight request, and Access-Control-Allow-Origin as key terms. L2 gives a simple-vs-non-simple-request table (method/headers/Content-Type), a mermaid sequence diagram of the preflight round-trip (OPTIONS → check → real request), a note that even simple requests get checked (just later, on the actual response), and a does-fetch()-reject table contrasting network/CORS failures (reject) against HTTP error statuses (resolve with `ok: false`). L3 implements and runs real JS: `needsPreflight(method, headers)` (verified GET+Authorization flips to `true` even though GET alone is simple), a `simulateCorsCheck` function reproducing the exact "blocked by CORS" failure against a `buggyConfig` missing the calling origin (verified it fails at the preflight step, never sending the real request) and its one-line fix via `fixedConfig` (verified it then passes), the same check against a simple request showing it's still blocked but only after reaching the server (verified, matching L1's server-logs-show-success detail), and `getUserBuggy`/`getUserFixed` demonstrating the fetch-resolves-on-404 gap and its `response.ok` fix — plus an extend-it question on distinguishing 401 from 404 and a 5-row failure-modes table.
- Wrote 24 exercises (8/6/10, 12 pools) — L1/L2 quiz-only, L3 mixing 4 code items across 2 pools (`cors-mechanics`: `needsPreflight`/`isSameOrigin`; `fetch-error-handling`: `simulateFetchResolution`/`checkResponseOk`) with 10 quiz items; all 4 code exercises' solutions and stubs independently verified against their exact tests via a standalone Node script before writing. Caught a real correctness risk before shipping: the sandbox's `ExercisePanel.astro` grading worker runs `eval()` fully synchronously with no `await` anywhere in its loop, so an `async`/`await` code exercise's assertions would never actually be awaited — a broken stub and a correct solution would both silently "pass." Confirmed this by reading `WORKER_SOURCE` directly, then rewrote what were originally `getUserBuggy`/`getUserFixed` async exercises as synchronous equivalents (`simulateFetchResolution`, `checkResponseOk`) that teach the identical lesson without needing `await` inside the graded code. Also caught and fixed one pool-consistency violation (`cors-mechanics-pool`'s two variants had different `reference` HTML) before finalizing.
- Built `interactives.json`: a `preflight-caching` demo (level 2) modeling 20 non-simple requests spaced 30 seconds apart, charting how many separate OPTIONS preflight round-trips actually occur as the `Access-Control-Max-Age` slider moves from 0 to 600 seconds — reproduces a clean, verified relationship (20 preflights at 0s caching, 10 at the default 60s, 1 at 600s). Verified in the browser across the full range.
- Ran the full guardrail suite (`generate:roadmap` — 218 units/16 tracks, `lint` — 0 issues, `format` — clean, `typecheck` — 0 errors, `validate:content` — 85 units checked, `test` — 9/9 pass, `build` — 103 pages) — clean.

Built around one running example across all three levels: two teams work on the same 6 hot files over the same 10-day sprint — Team Gitflow keeps one branch open the whole sprint and hits 4 conflicting files at once on merge day; Team Trunk merges daily and hits zero conflicts across the entire sprint, despite writing roughly the same amount of code.

L2 gives a mermaid flowchart showing a conflict already existing by day 2 of divergence (only _discovered_ later at merge time), a trunk-based-vs-gitflow trade-off table (branch lifetime, when conflicts are discovered, total conflict volume, feature-flag requirement, per-merge cost), and a section on why merge cost tracks time-unreconciled rather than code volume. L3 implements a seeded-PRNG simulation (`simulateTrunkBased`, `simulateGitflow`) reproducing the exact scenario — verified 0 conflicts for trunk-based and 4 for gitflow at 10 days with the same seed and identical underlying random draws — then extends across 5/10/20/30-day sprints, revealing gitflow's conflict count is bounded by the 6-file set (so the gap narrows to nearly equal, 5 vs. 6, at 30 days) while trunk-based's has no such ceiling — a genuine nuance the naive "trunk-based always wins" framing would have missed, caught only by actually running the extended simulation rather than asserting it.

## 2026-08-19 — New unit: applied-math/queueing-theory-basics

- Auto-picked `applied-math/queueing-theory-basics` — lowest done/total ratio (6/15, 40.0%) — "How do I predict how a system behaves under load before it's built? (queueing theory basics: Little's Law, latency vs. throughput)".
- Technical, domain-specific unit — grounded directly in real code throughout. Built around one running example across all three levels: a checkout service load-tested at 60% capacity (250ms average) looks fine, but at 90% capacity (only 50% more traffic) response time jumps to over 1,000ms — a 4x increase from a 1.5x traffic increase.
- L1 introduces arrival rate, service rate, utilization, Little's Law, and latency-vs-throughput as key terms. L2 gives an `xychart-beta` of the wait-time-vs-utilization curve (flat 10%-50%, sharply rising 70%-95%), a mechanistic explanation of why the curve bends (a server with no slack left to absorb bursts near 100% utilization), a mermaid flowchart of Little's Law's three related quantities, and a section on why throughput and latency can diverge. L3 builds a real, seeded-PRNG discrete-event queue simulation (Mulberry32 + exponential interarrival/service times) and node-verifies it against the closed-form M/M/1 formulas at 5 utilization levels (20%–90%) — confirming an 8x wait-time increase from only 4.5x more arrivals — then reuses the exact λ=6/λ=9 rows to reproduce the opening scenario's own 250ms/1,000ms+ numbers, closing with a "what generalizes" section (the qualitative knee-shaped curve generalizes; the exact numbers are specific to the single-server M/M/1 model) and an extend-it question about adding a second server.
- Wrote 38 exercises (11/13/14, 17 pools) — L1/L2 quiz-only, L3 mixing 4 code items across 2 pools (`theoretical-mm1`: `theoreticalMM1`/`mm1Metrics` variants implementing the closed-form ρ/W/L formulas; `littles-law`: `littlesLaw`/`avgInSystem` variants implementing L=λW) with 10 quiz items. All 4 code exercises' solutions node-verified against their exact tests before writing. Came in slightly below the ~40 target (38) — noted explicitly rather than padded.
- **Caught and fixed a real, previously-undetected correctness bug in the exercise-grading sandbox pattern**, discovered while writing this unit's multi-assertion tests: `ExercisePanel.astro`'s `expect(...).toBe(...)` returns `undefined` on success (only throws on failure) — so a test written as `return expect(a).toBe(b) && expect(c).toBe(d)` short-circuits after the first assertion (since `undefined` is falsy) and **never evaluates the second assertion at all**, silently letting a wrong second value pass.
