- Auto-picked `logic/edge-case-reasoning` — lowest done/total ratio (5/12, 41.7%) — "How do I prove my code handles every case, not just the ones I tested? (edge case reasoning, invariants)".
- Technical, domain-specific unit — grounded directly in real code throughout. Built around one running example across all three levels: a three-tier shipping-cost function whose developer wrote one test per tier (all interior values), all passed, but a real customer package weighing exactly 5.0kg got overcharged because the code used `weight < 5` while the business rule meant "5kg and under."
- L1 introduces edge case, boundary condition, equivalence class, invariant, and off-by-one error as key terms via a `Scenario` callout. L2 gives a systematic-process mermaid flowchart (identify input space → partition into equivalence classes → identify boundaries → test boundary ± one step → test out-of-domain values), a number-line flowchart pinpointing the bug at exactly one point, an edge-case-category table (zero, negative, exact boundary, just-past-boundary, maximum, empty — each with why it's typically skipped), and a section proving a plausible monotonicity invariant ("cost never decreases") holds true for both the buggy and fixed versions, since the bug still moves the price _up_ at the miscategorized boundary. L3 implements and node-verifies the buggy function, directly tests the monotonicity invariant and confirms it does NOT catch the bug, then builds and verifies the boundary-value test suite (threshold ± one step) that does, plus separate domain-check tests (negative/zero) — closing with a "what generalizes" section and an extend-it question about a rate-change boundary needing an invariant-style check instead of a fixed-value comparison.

- **Caught and fixed a real syntax bug during authoring**: several newly-written test `expr` strings used a bare `return expect(...).toBe(...);` without wrapping in an IIFE — since `ExercisePanel.astro`'s worker runs `eval(code + "\n" + t.expr)` at the top level (not inside a function), a bare `return` is an illegal syntax error. Fixed by stripping the `return` keyword to match the plain-expression convention used elsewhere in the codebase (e.g. `algorithmic-complexity`'s exercises), then verified the exact eval mechanism against all six fixed expressions in a standalone Node reproduction before trusting them in the browser.
- `bun run check`'s first pass also caught a pool-consistency violation (`boundary-fix-pool`'s two variants had different `reference` HTML, referencing their own specific operators) — fixed by aligning both to shared, operator-agnostic wording.
- Built `interactives.json`: a `buggy-vs-fixed-tier` demo (level 2) — a `weightKg` slider (0–20kg) computing both the buggy (`<`) and fixed (`<=`) tier price live — default (5.0kg) reproduces the opening scenario's exact divergence ($8.99 buggy vs. $4.99 fixed); verified in the browser that the two lines agree everywhere except exactly at the boundary.

## 2026-08-20 — New unit: testing-quality/flaky-tests

- Auto-picked `testing-quality/flaky-tests` — lowest done/total ratio across tracks (6/13, 46.2%) — "Why does my test suite pass locally but fail in CI? (flaky tests, non-determinism, environment leakage)".
- Technical track (rule 9) — grounded in real JavaScript throughout. Built around one running example across all three levels: a 40-test suite passes 100/100 times locally but fails about 1 in 20 times in CI with no code changes, traced to two tests sharing a module-level `orderStore` array that neither resets.
- L1 introduces non-determinism, test isolation, environment leakage, and race condition (in a test) as key terms via a `Scenario` callout. L2 gives a table of the four main flakiness causes (shared state, real time, real randomness, unresolved async work) and a mermaid flowchart explaining why CI's shuffled/parallel execution surfaces order-dependent bugs that a single developer's fixed local run order hides. L3 walks the shared-store bug through a full fix (per-test `makeStore()` instead of a shared array) and a second common cause, an unawaited async operation racing its own assertion — closing with a generalization section, an extend-it question about whether fixing only the await (not the shared store) would be enough if both bugs coexisted, and a four-row failure-modes table.
- Wrote 22 exercises (10/8/4, 11 pools) — quiz for L1/L2, code for L3 per rule 9 (technical track). Slightly below the ~40 target with only 2 L3 code pools — noted explicitly rather than padded.
- **Discovered and worked around a genuine test-harness limitation this unit surfaced for the first time**: the exercise sandbox's `runInWorker` (`ExercisePanel.astro`) calls `eval(code + "\n" + expr)` synchronously and pushes `passed: true` immediately without awaiting any returned Promise — so a code exercise whose `expr` uses top-level `await` throws a SyntaxError (confirmed via a standalone Node reproduction of the exact eval context: `await` at the top level of a non-async function is invalid), and an `expr` that instead returns a Promise via `.then()` (the pattern already used in `web/client-side-caching`) gets marked "passed" immediately regardless of whether the promise ever actually resolves or rejects — meaning a buggy, unawaited-async starter implementation would incorrectly show as passing. Rather than ship a code exercise that always shows green regardless of correctness, replaced the originally-planned `await-fix-pool` (testing the unawaited-promise race fix) with a `fixed-clock-pool` testing the same L2 "real time" flakiness category via a fully synchronous, properly-gradable pattern instead (inject `now` as a parameter instead of reading `Date.now()` internally) — verified the replacement's stub correctly fails and solution correctly passes via the exact eval mechanism. The async race-condition example stays in L3 as verified narrative content (its code was independently confirmed correct in a standalone Node script) but intentionally has no attached browser-graded exercise, since the harness can't grade it reliably.
- Built `interactives.json` (level 1, one demo): sliders for a test's per-run failure percentage and number of CI runs, computing the probability of seeing at least one failure across N independent runs (`1 - (1-p)^n`) — real, verified probability math tied directly to the unit's "1 in 20 CI runs" framing, not a fabricated coefficient.
- Ran the full guardrail suite (`generate:roadmap` — 218 units/16 tracks, `lint` — 0 issues, `format:check` — clean, `typecheck` — 0 errors, `validate:content` — 110 units checked, `test` — 9/9 pass, `build` — 128 pages) — clean.
- Chrome extension bridge connected — full browser verification via `astro preview --background`: L1/L2/L3 all render; the interactive demo confirmed showing 64.2% at its default (5%, 20 runs), matching the verified formula exactly, and confirmed correct at both extremes (1% → 1%, 50%/200 runs → 100%); L2's CI-vs-local mermaid flowchart confirmed full text via `foreignObject` extraction; L1's exercise panel confirmed showing 2 of 5 pools; one quiz item answered incorrectly on purpose confirmed radios locked plus "Not quite" feedback, another answered correctly confirmed "Correct" feedback; L3 confirmed rendering with 2 code pools shown — ran a full code-exercise cycle on `isolated-store`: stub code correctly fails the "doesn't affect a different store" test (✗) while passing the other two, pasting the revealed solution and re-running shows all three tests passing (✓).

- Deliberately omitted `interactives.json` — XSS defense correctness is fundamentally binary (escaped/not escaped) rather than a continuous quantity, no natural slider relationship exists here without fabricating one.
- **Found and fixed a real, pre-existing bug in `ExercisePanel.astro` during browser verification, not content-specific to this unit**: `set:html={JSON.stringify(pool.variants)}` injected raw JSON directly into an inline `<script type="application/json">` tag with no escaping. Any exercise whose text contains a literal `</script` substring — inevitable here, since this unit's own test payloads are literal strings like `"<script>alert(1)</script>"` — closes the `<script>` tag early per the HTML spec (which recognizes `</script` inside any script element regardless of type or string-literal context), corrupting the rest of the page's DOM. In the browser this reproducibly froze the entire tab's renderer thread hard enough that `get_page_text`, `javascript_tool`, and even `read_network_requests` all timed out after 45s — confirmed content-specific by loading other already-shipped units successfully on the same preview server and extension session in between failed attempts, and confirmed the mermaid diagram itself (tested standalone) was not the cause. Fixed by escaping `</script` to `<\/script` before injecting (`.replace(/<\/script/gi, "<\\/script")`) — the standard technique, since `\/` is valid JSON and decodes back to `/` on `JSON.parse`. This is a general component-level bug, not unique to this unit — any future unit's code exercises testing HTML-escaping or similar could have hit the same freeze; worth keeping in mind if a new unit's preview hangs identically.
- Re-ran the full guardrail suite after the component fix (`generate:roadmap` — 218 units/16 tracks, `lint` — 0 issues, `format:check` — clean, `typecheck` — 0 errors, `validate:content` — 122 units checked, `test` — 9/9 pass, `build` — 140 pages) — clean.
- Chrome extension bridge connected — full browser verification via `astro preview --background` (after the fix, page loads normally): L1 renders with the Scenario callout and exercise panel showing "2 of 5"; L2's reflected-vs-stored mermaid diagram confirmed rendering (14 text/foreignObject nodes) after tab switch; one quiz item answered incorrectly on purpose confirmed radios locked plus "Not quite" feedback with explanation, three others answered correctly confirmed "Correct" feedback with explanation; L3 confirmed rendering with 2 code pools — ran a full code-exercise cycle on `escape-html-pool`: stub code correctly fails all 3 tests, pasting the revealed solution and re-running shows all three tests passing, with `exercise:web/xss/escape-html-pool` persisted as `"passed"` in `localStorage`.
- Flipped `web/xss` to `done` in `ROADMAP.md`. 122 total `done` units now.

## 2026-08-20 — Site-wide visuals pilot: 4 phases

- User flagged the site was missing images and asked to pilot four phases of visual work (not a content unit — cross-cutting site infra/design, scoped to a pilot before any full rollout).
- **Phase 1 — OG/social preview images.** Added `src/lib/og-image.ts` (satori for layout + `@resvg/resvg-js` for PNG rasterization; `wawoff2` to decompress `@fontsource/inter`'s woff2 files since satori only reads ttf/otf/woff) and two static endpoints, `src/pages/og/[track].png.ts` and `src/pages/og/[track]/[unit].png.ts`, generating a 1200×630 PNG per track/unit page. Wired `og:image`/`twitter:image` meta tags into `Layout.astro` via a new `ogImage` prop. No production `site` is configured in `astro.config.mjs` yet, so the resolved URL falls back to Astro's build-time placeholder origin — noted inline for whoever sets up deployment to fix by adding `site:`.
- **Phase 2 — SVG illustration pilot.** Built `src/components/UnitIllustration.astro`: hand-authored inline SVG hero illustrations, outline style matching `lucide-astro` (stroke-based, currentColor, sky accent matching the existing mermaid chart palette), keyed by unit slug. Piloted and shipped for all 10 `web/` units (`http-request-response-basics`, `cors`, `xss`, `bundling`, `client-side-caching`, `component-state`, `css-cascade-specificity`, `dom-event-model`, `need-html-semantics-just-divs`, `render-performance`). Hit and fixed one real bug: Astro scopes component `<style>` blocks by rewriting the template's static markup with a hash class at build time, which never reaches content injected via `set:html` at runtime — every rule touching the injected SVG had to be wrapped in `:global()` (scoped under a `.unit-illustration` class to avoid leaking `.label`/`.accent` globally) or it silently never matched.
- **Phase 3 — real screenshot pilot (`web/xss`).** Generated two tiny static HTML pages server-side (mirroring the unit's exact `renderCommentVulnerable`/`escapeHtml`/`renderCommentFixed` functions) so the malicious `<script>` was present at initial parse time — script tags inserted later via `innerHTML`/`outerHTML` are inert and don't execute, which the first attempt at this demo hit and had to correct. Also hit and fixed a real data-safety issue: the first working version read `document.cookie` to "prove" the exploit, which picked up real session/auth cookies from another local app sharing the same browser profile (cookies are scoped by host, not port) — replaced with a hardcoded placeholder string before any screenshot was kept, and deleted the screenshot taken before the fix. Captured both pages via `claude-in-chrome`, cropped/stacked into one comparison image with ImageMagick, saved to `src/content/web/xss/images/vulnerable-vs-escaped.png`, and embedded via a normal relative Markdown image in `L3-deep-dive.md` — Astro's built-in content-collection image pipeline picked it up automatically (270kB PNG → 39kB webp at build).
- **Phase 4 — interactives.json for `web/xss`.** Added an L1-level slider demo, `xss-exposure-window`, grounded in the unit's own Scenario (the comment page's malicious script runs in every visitor's session until it's caught) rather than an abstract example: `dailyVisitors` (10–2000) × `daysToFix` (0–14) → `exposedSessions`, chart swept across `daysToFix`. Verified in-browser by dragging both sliders to their min and max (10×14=140, 10×0=0 — both correct) per the interactive-demos testing requirement, since `validate:content` can't check `compute` actually runs correctly.
- Ran `bun run check` after each phase (generate:roadmap → lint → format:check → typecheck → validate:content → test → build) — clean after minor fixes each time: two Prettier reformats, and a TypeScript fight in the OG endpoints where Node's `Buffer`/`Uint8Array` (typed over `ArrayBufferLike`, which includes `SharedArrayBuffer`) doesn't satisfy DOM's `BodyInit`/`BlobPart` types (which want a real `ArrayBuffer`) — resolved by having `renderOgImage` return a `.slice()`-copied `ArrayBuffer` directly instead of fighting the wrapper types.
- New dependencies added: `satori`, `@resvg/resvg-js`, `@fontsource/inter`, `wawoff2`.
- Not yet done, scoped as explicit future work per the pilot-before-rollout structure: illustrations/screenshots/interactives for the other 15 tracks (115 units), and setting a real `site` in `astro.config.mjs` once this deploys somewhere.
- Next: user to review the pilot's remaining open item (xss illustration composition was flagged then re-reviewed and accepted as-is) and decide whether/how to scale Phases 2–4 beyond `web/`.

User asked for three site-wide changes in one session: a more inviting homepage, a way to find roadmap units by need (not just a flat list), and a distinct accent color per level (L1/L2/L3) instead of one shared accent. Asked to sequence the work fastest-to-slowest and defer the slowest piece to a future session rather than do it now.

**Done, in order:**

1. **Per-level accent colors** ("semáforo temático": L1 = blue/sky — the site's original accent, kept as the `:root` default so nothing outside a level context regresses; L2 = emerald; L3 = amber). Implemented as CSS custom properties (`--accent`, `--accent-text`, `--accent-soft-bg`, `--accent-soft-border`) in `src/styles/global.css`, overridden per level via `[data-tab-panel="l2"/"l3"]` (ancestor cascade, covers anything rendered inside a level's tab panel) and `[data-tab-button="l2"/"l3"]` (the tab buttons themselves, which sit outside any single panel). Consumers switched from hardcoded sky hex/Tailwind classes to these tokens: `UnitIllustration.astro`'s `.accent`/`.accent-stroke`/`.accent-text` style rules, `.prose-panel`'s bold/link colors and inline-code chip, `Scenario.astro`'s callout background/border/label (now a `.scenario-box`/`.scenario-box-label` pair of classes instead of inline `sky-*` Tailwind classes), `LevelTabs.astro`'s active-tab underline/text, and `InteractiveDemo.astro`'s range-slider `accent-color`. Verified visually in a real browser (not just build/typecheck): switching L1→L2→L3 on `web/xss` visibly recolors headings, code highlights, the Scenario callout, and the tab underline to blue/emerald/amber respectively, in dark mode.
2. **Homepage redesign** (`src/pages/index.astro`, full rewrite): hero headline + subhead, a build-time stat row (units written/total, tracks started, % of roadmap — computed from existing `getWrittenUnits()`/`roadmap` data, no new data source), a search box that GETs to `/roadmap?search=...`, a client-side "continue where you left off" callout that scans `localStorage`'s `progress:*` keys (written by `ProgressToggle`/`spaced-repetition.ts`) and links to the most relevant not-yet-done unit (graceful no-op for first-time visitors), and richer hover micro-interactions on the track cards (lift + shadow + staggered fade-in reusing the existing `animate-fade-slide-in` keyframe). ## 2026-08-21 — Manual dark/light theme toggle

User noticed the site had no way to switch theme manually — it only ever followed `prefers-color-scheme`, with `dark:` Tailwind utilities used throughout every component. Added a real sun/moon toggle.

- **`src/styles/global.css`**: added `@custom-variant dark (&:where(.dark, .dark *));` — this is the standard Tailwind v4 way to switch every existing `dark:` utility site-wide from following the OS media query to following a `.dark` class on `<html>`, with no per-component changes needed. Also switched this file's own hand-written `@media (prefers-color-scheme: dark) { ... }` blocks (the per-level accent-token overrides from the earlier accent-color work) to plain `.dark`/`:root.dark` selectors, so the accent tokens stay in sync with the same toggle.
- **`src/layouts/Layout.astro`**:
  - Added a blocking `<script is:inline>` as the very first thing in `<head>` (before any CSS/content) that resolves `localStorage.getItem("theme")` (explicit prior choice) falling back to `matchMedia("(prefers-color-scheme: dark)")` (system default) into a `.dark` class on `<html>` — must run this early/synchronously to avoid a flash of the wrong theme on load.
  - Added a sun/moon `<button data-theme-toggle>` in the header nav (next to the roadmap link) — both `Sun` and `Moon` (lucide-astro) are rendered, one hidden via `dark:` utility classes so the _other_ theme's icon shows (i.e. shows the icon for what clicking it would switch _to_).
  - Click handler: flips the stored preference in `localStorage` and calls `location.reload()` rather than live-toggling the class — chosen deliberately because already-rendered Mermaid diagrams bake their theme colors into SVG at render time (see the existing `chartThemeVariables` logic), so a live class flip would leave any diagram on the page showing stale colors until the next navigation anyway. A reload is simpler and fully correct; acceptable since this is a content site, not an app.
  - Updated the existing mermaid-theming script's `prefersDark` to read `document.documentElement.classList.contains("dark")` instead of calling `matchMedia` directly — otherwise a manual override to dark on a light-OS machine (or vice versa) would render mermaid charts in the wrong theme even though the rest of the page was correct.
- **Real bug caught and fixed before this shipped**: the `bun run check` run right after these edits reported exit code 0, but that was a false positive — it was invoked as `bun run check | tail -100`, and piping through `tail` makes the shell report `tail`'s exit code, not the actual check's. The real run had failed at `format:check` (an unrelated `PROGRESS.md` formatting issue from this same session) and never reached `typecheck`/`build`, so the preview server briefly served a stale `dist/` with none of these changes (confirmed missing `data-theme-toggle` in the served HTML). Fixed by formatting `PROGRESS.md` and re-running `bun run check` **without** a trailing pipe so the real exit code was visible (`echo "EXIT_CODE=$?"` after it) — this time genuinely exit 0, full build included. **Lesson for future sessions: never pipe `bun run check`'s output through `tail`/`head`/etc. without separately capturing and checking its real exit code — do `bun run check; echo "EXIT_CODE=$?"` or check `$PIPESTATUS[0]` if piping is truly needed.**
- Verified in-browser end-to-end after rebuilding: toggle correctly flips light↔dark, persists via `localStorage` across a real navigation (homepage → roadmap), and the early head script prevents a flash on reload.
- Ran `bun run check` again (real exit code checked directly this time) — clean.

**Deferred (unchanged, still pending from earlier entries):** the tag taxonomy + tag-chip roadmap facet, and the per-unit myth/reality extension noted above.

## 2026-08-21 — Local "profile" / session system + onboarding gate

User asked for the site to gate itself behind a lightweight session on first visit — pick a name, login/register-style selector if a profile already exists locally, per-profile progress, a way to switch or delete a profile (deleting also wipes its data), and a short explainer of what junior/mid/senior/staff/principal mean. Explicitly local-only: no backend, no real auth, just named buckets in the browser so more than one person can use the same machine.

- **`src/lib/profile.ts`** (new): the whole model. A `Profile` is `{id, name, createdAt}` stored in a flat `profiles` array under an un-namespaced `localStorage` key, plus a separate `activeProfileId` key. `getProfiles`/`getActiveProfile`/`createProfile`/`switchProfile`/`deleteProfile` are the CRUD surface; `deleteProfile` also sweeps every `localStorage` key prefixed `p:<id>:` so deleting a profile actually erases its data, not just its name from the list. The real design decision is **`pStorage`**: a drop-in `{getItem, setItem, removeItem, keys}` replacement for `localStorage` that transparently prefixes every call with `p:<activeProfileId>:` — every module that stores _personal_ data (progress, exercise pass/fail, whiteboard drawings) was switched from raw `localStorage` to `pStorage` instead of hand-rolling prefixing at each call site. It silently no-ops (empty reads, dropped writes) when there's no active profile yet, rather than throwing — `ProgressToggle`/`ExercisePanel`/the homepage's "continue learning" script all run their init unconditionally on page load regardless of whether the onboarding gate is currently blocking the user, so a hard failure there would break the page; a profile is always chosen (and the page reloaded) before any of that state matters for real.
- **Left deliberately on raw `localStorage`, not `pStorage`**: `theme` (device-level preference, not personal progress) and `whiteboard:board-style` (white/green — a UI preference, shared across profiles on purpose, per the existing whiteboard design). Only `whiteboard.ts`'s actual per-drawing canvas data (`opts.storageKey`), `spaced-repetition.ts`'s progress/exercise keys, and `index.astro`'s "continue learning" scan were switched to `pStorage`.
- **`src/components/ProfileGate.astro`** (new): a single shared full-screen modal, mounted once in `Layout.astro` right after `<main>`. Forces itself open (no Cancel button) whenever `getActiveProfile()` is null on page load — this is what makes picking a profile unavoidable on first visit, in any browser tab, on any page. Also reopens in dismissible mode (Cancel visible) whenever a `profile-gate:open` `CustomEvent` fires. Content: a short "what is this site" paragraph plus a compact `Junior`/`Mid`/`Senior`/`Staff`/`Principal` glossary (site chrome, not unit content — the project's rule against using those words as a classification device applies inside units, not to a meta explainer like this one) above the actual picker — existing profiles render as "Continue as X" rows (click → `switchProfile` + `location.reload()`, matching the existing pattern the theme toggle uses for correctness after a state change) each with its own delete (trash icon) button. Delete is a **two-step inline confirm** (first click arms it — red highlight + `aria-label` changes to "Confirm delete X" for 3s, second click within that window actually deletes) rather than a native `confirm()` dialog, consistent with the project's existing avoidance of blocking browser dialogs elsewhere.
- **`src/layouts/Layout.astro`**: added a small pill button in the header nav (next to the theme toggle) showing the active profile's name (`UserRound` icon + name, updated client-side since the name isn't known at build/server time); clicking it dispatches `profile-gate:open` to reopen the switcher. Rendered `<ProfileGate />` once, site-wide.
- **`src/lib/spaced-repetition.ts`**, **`src/lib/whiteboard.ts`**, **`src/pages/index.astro`**: swapped their direct `localStorage.getItem/setItem/removeItem`/`Object.keys(localStorage)` calls for the `pStorage` equivalents (see above for what stayed raw).
- Ran `bun run check` (generate:roadmap → lint → format:check → typecheck → validate:content → test → build) — one Prettier reformat needed on `ProfileGate.astro`, clean on the second run (218 units, 143 pages built). Real exit code checked directly, not through a pipe (per the standing lesson from an earlier session).
- Browser-verified via `astro preview --background` (screenshots hit the same intermittent CDP `Page.captureScreenshot` timeout noted in prior sessions on this machine; fell back to `javascript_tool` DOM/localStorage inspection throughout, which is authoritative anyway): fresh browser state correctly forced the gate open with no Cancel button; created profile "Franco" via real click+type interaction, gate closed, header pill updated to "Franco"; seeded progress for Franco, created a second profile "Ale" via the header trigger (gate reopened with Cancel visible, listing Franco), confirmed Ale started with zero visibility into Franco's progress; switched back to Franco via the picker and confirmed on a real unit page that "Mark as done" correctly showed "Done" again; exercised the two-step delete confirm on Ale (first click arms/highlights without deleting, second click within the window actually removes the profile and clears its data — my first attempt across separate tool calls missed the 3s window and correctly re-armed instead of deleting, which is exactly the intended safety behavior, not a bug); confirmed Cancel closes the gate without changing the active profile.
- **Hand-off, explicitly requested by the user**: a future session should build **roadmap personalization** on top of this — let a profile pick which topics/tags it cares about (the tag taxonomy from the "Tag taxonomy + tag-chip roadmap facet" session above already gives a shared vocabulary in `roadmap.json`'s `tags[]`) and use that to filter or reorder the homepage track grid and/or `roadmap.astro`. `Profile` in `profile.ts` already has a comment marking the intended extension point (`interests?: string[]`). Suggested shape: add `interests: string[]` to `Profile`, a small settings UI (could live inside `ProfileGate` as a per-profile step, or as its own trigger next to the header pill) to pick tags from the existing vocabulary, persist via `switchProfile`-adjacent helpers in `profile.ts`, and consume `getActiveProfile()?.interests` wherever `roadmap.astro`/`index.astro` render tracks or the tag-chip facet — e.g. pre-select matching tag chips, or sort/badge tracks that match. Not started this session; this paragraph is the hand-off.
- Did not touch `ROADMAP.md` — this is a site feature (chrome/infrastructure), not a curriculum unit, so no roadmap row applies.

## 2026-08-21 — Roadmap personalization (interests)

Picked up the hand-off from the prior entry.

- **`src/lib/profile.ts`**: added `interests?: string[]` to `Profile` (replacing the old placeholder comment) plus `updateProfileInterests(id, interests)` to persist a full replacement list for a profile.
- **`src/components/ProfileGate.astro`**: added an interest-picker step using the existing 47/48-tag vocabulary (read from `roadmap.json` at build time via `roadmap.ts`, same source `roadmap.astro`'s facet uses, embedded as a `<script type="application/json">` payload for the client script to read). Two entry points, per the brief: (1) right after creating a new profile — `submitNewProfile()` no longer reloads immediately; it hides the name/list UI and shows a chip picker + "Continue" button, which reloads once clicked (optional — clicking Continue with nothing selected is a normal skip); (2) reopening the gate via the header profile pill when a profile is already active shows the same chip picker pre-populated with that profile's current interests, editable inline, with Cancel available. Each chip click calls `updateProfileInterests` immediately (no separate save step), reusing the same chip visual/`aria-pressed` pattern as `roadmap.astro`'s tag facet.
- **`src/pages/roadmap.astro`**: client script now imports `getActiveProfile` and, on init, pre-selects (sets `aria-pressed` + adds to the `activeTags` filter set) any tag chip matching the active profile's interests before the first `applyFilters()` call. Purely a starting point — the reader can deselect/clear same as any other filter state. No interests → no chips pre-selected, identical to pre-existing behavior.
- **`src/pages/index.astro`**: each track card gets a `data-tags` attribute (the union of tags across that track's units, computed at build time) and a small hidden `Sparkles`-icon "For you" badge. A new client script (`initInterestPersonalization`, wire-once via `grid.dataset.wired`) reads `getActiveProfile()?.interests`; if empty, it does nothing and returns early (default order, no badges — unchanged from before this session). If non-empty, it un-hides the badge on every card whose tag set intersects the interest set and re-appends matched cards before unmatched ones (re-appending an already-attached node moves it in place, no markup duplication) — matched tracks float to the top, in their original relative order, unmatched tracks follow in their original relative order.
- Ran `bun run check` (generate:roadmap → lint → format:check → typecheck → validate:content → test → build) — one Prettier reformat needed on `ProfileGate.astro`, clean on the second run (exit code 0 checked directly from the background task's own exit capture, not piped through `tail`/`head`; 218 units, 143 pages built).
- Browser-verified via `astro preview --background` (screenshots hit the same intermittent CDP `Page.captureScreenshot` timeout as prior sessions on this machine; fell back to `javascript_tool` DOM/`localStorage` inspection throughout): fresh state still forces the plain onboarding gate with the interests section hidden (no active profile yet); created a profile, confirmed the create flow stepped into the interest picker (create row hidden, 48 chips rendered, Continue visible) rather than reloading immediately; clicked `algorithms` + `api-design`, confirmed `localStorage.profiles` updated with `interests` immediately (before clicking Continue); clicked Continue → reloaded → homepage reordered matched tracks (`web`, `systems`, `logic`, `applied-math`, `testing-quality`, `software-design`) to the front with visible "For you" badges, unmatched tracks followed in original order; `/roadmap` pre-selected exactly the `algorithms`/`api-design` chips and correctly filtered to 14/218 matching units; reopened the gate via the header pill and confirmed it showed the interest picker pre-populated with both selections and a working Cancel button (edit-later entry point); deselected both chips, confirmed `interests` persisted as `[]`, and confirmed both pages reverted exactly to their pre-personalization default behavior (roadmap: 218/218 unfiltered, no chips pressed; homepage: original track order, no badges visible) — the "optional, no forced choice, no regression when empty" requirement.

## 2026-08-21 — Personalization follow-up: hide non-matching tracks, add salary bands

User feedback after the prior entry: (1) the homepage's interest personalization only reordered/badged tracks — the user expected non-matching tracks to not appear at all once a profile has interests set, unless the profile's interests are updated; (2) the onboarding glossary (Junior/Mid/Senior/Staff/Principal) was missing typical salary differences between those levels.

- **`src/pages/index.astro`**: `initInterestPersonalization` changed from reorder-only to hide-non-matching — when the active profile has any interests, every track card whose tag set doesn't intersect them gets `classList.add("hidden")` outright instead of just being pushed down; matching cards keep the existing "For you" badge. ## 2026-08-27 — Two new content-pacing components: `LevelIntro` and `Checkpoint`

User asked what could make a unit page feel less like a dump of information. Landed on two additions: (1) `LevelTabs.astro` now tracks per-level read state (a checkmark once a level's end sentinel scrolls into view), remembers the last level viewed across visits, and adds a "Next level" button at the end of L1/L2 — all `localStorage`-only, no gating effect. (2) Two new ungraded pacing components for mid-content pauses: `LevelIntro.astro` (a 3-item "in this level" callout right after a level's opening hook resolves) and `Checkpoint.astro` (a mid-content active-recall question with a "Show answer" reveal toggle). Both read the project's existing per-level accent tokens (`--accent`/`--accent-soft-bg`/etc.) automatically, no extra styling needed per unit.

- Fixed a real `IntersectionObserver` bug found during browser verification: a hidden (`display:none`) tab panel's sentinel collapses to a zero-size rect at `(0,0)`, which Chrome still reported as `isIntersecting: true` against the viewport's top-left — falsely marking a just-switched-to level as already read. Fixed with an `offsetParent === null` guard.
- Documented both new components in `CLAUDE.md` (new "Checkpoints and level intros" section, plus references from rule 5c's per-unit checklist and the session workflow).
- **Piloted `LevelIntro`/`Checkpoint` on `web/http-request-response-basics`** (all 3 levels — L1/L2/L3 `LevelIntro`, 1 `Checkpoint` in L2, 2 in L3), converting `L2-concept.md` and `L3-deep-dive.md` to `.mdx`.
- **Tested whether the new CLAUDE.md wording alone (no hand-holding) was sufficient** by having a subagent retrofit the same pattern onto the already-written `git-teamwork/merge-rebase` unit from CLAUDE.md's instructions only. It succeeded (`bun run check` clean, correct rendering/toggle behavior verified in `astro preview`) but surfaced 2 real gaps: (1) the cited worked example (`http-request-response-basics`) was itself incomplete at pilot time — only L1 had been touched, so the agent had to invent unaddressed patterns itself; (2) CLAUDE.md's rule didn't say what to do for a level with no `<Scenario>` hook (a bare `LevelIntro` at file-top would violate "never at the very top"). Fixed both: finished the `http-request-response-basics` pilot across all 3 levels so the cited example is now actually complete, and added an explicit bridging-sentence instruction to CLAUDE.md's `LevelIntro` bullet for hook-less levels, plus a concrete "end of a resolved `##` section, only where the next section depends on it" heuristic to the `Checkpoint` placement bullet (previously just "where a concept just landed," which the agent flagged as judgment-only).
- Ran `bun run check` after combining both units' changes — clean (158 pages built).
- Browser/`curl`-verified rendered HTML for both units: `web/http-request-response-basics` has 3 `LevelIntro`s + 3 `Checkpoint`s, `git-teamwork/merge-rebase` has 3 `LevelIntro`s + 4 `Checkpoint`s — matching what was authored in each.
- Not yet done: rolling this out to the other ~156 written units. That's a separate, larger decision (per-unit retrofit cost) the user hasn't greenlit yet — these two units exist as the validated reference pair for whenever that rollout happens.

## 2026-08-28 — Full `LevelIntro`/`Checkpoint` rollout: all 133 remaining written units, batches 1–19

User greenlit the batched rollout across every remaining written unit: dispatch parallel subagents in batches of 7 (one unit each, no self-verification), coordinator runs `bun run format` + a `</content>`-artifact grep + `bun run check` + a commit after every batch. Ran to completion across 19 batches this session, covering all 133 units left after the 4 pilots + `build-vs-buy`:

- **Batches 1–4** (28 units): `applied-math` (7), `applied-math`/`architecture`/`business-communication` mix (7), `business-communication`/`career-craft` (7), `career-craft` (7).
- **Batch 5** (`corporate-politics`, 9 units) hit an account-wide session-limit error mid-run, leaving 3 units partially edited and 4 fully untouched; verified the partial work was well-formed (not corrupted), fixed one duplicate `.md`/`.mdx` pair, committed the partial state per explicit user instruction, then finished the remaining work in a follow-up batch once the limit reset.
- **Batches 6–19** (remaining ~96 units): `design`, `git-teamwork`, `infra-delivery`, `learning-craft`, `logic`, `people-management`, `product-domain`, `security`, `software-design`, `sustainable-performance`, `systems`, `testing-quality`, `web` — one batch of 7 per track segment, in the order tracks happened to sort, no unit skipped.
- **Two more account session-limit interruptions** occurred mid-rollout (batch 11/`people-management` and batch 17/`systems`+`testing-quality`), each leaving a mix of fully-done, partially-done, and untouched units in the batch. Same recovery pattern each time: read `git status` and grep each unit's files for `LevelIntro` count to distinguish complete/partial/untouched, discard or finish dangling half-edits (one file had a dangling import with no component body; deleted the stray line rather than the whole file), redispatch only the unfinished units, then re-verify and commit.
- **Two real recurring bugs found and fixed across the rollout, both now called out explicitly in every subsequent agent's prompt as a standing self-check:**
  1. **Stray trailing `</content>` line** — an artifact some agents' file-writing tool leaves at the end of an edited file with no matching opening tag, breaking `astro build`'s MDX parser. First seen in the original 2-pilot session, recurred at least twice more in this rollout (once in a `git-teamwork` unit from batch 7, unrelated to that batch's own edits, and once in `testing-quality/bdd` from batch 17's redispatch). Fix is always the same: delete the stray trailing line.
  2. **Backslash-escaped double quotes inside a JSX attribute** — `question="...\"...\""` inside a `<Checkpoint>` is invalid JSX (unlike a JS string literal, JSX attribute strings don't support backslash escapes) and broke `astro build` with an opaque `Unexpected character after <` error that named no file or line number directly usable from the CLI output. Root-caused by writing a one-off script that ran every `.mdx` file in `src/content/` through `@mdx-js/mdx`'s `compile()` directly, which does report the failing file. Found in `git-teamwork/snapshots-manual-copies/L3-deep-dive.mdx` (a pre-existing bug from batch 7, undetected until this later re-verification), fixed by switching to single quotes for the inner quoted text. From batch 17 onward, every agent prompt explicitly warns about this pattern and instructs a self-check grep for it before finishing.
- Every batch's coordinator verification step was: `bun run format` → `grep -rl "</content>" src/content/` → (from batch 17 on) `grep -rn 'question="[^"]*\\\\"'` → `bun run check` (full `astro build`, 159 pages) → `git add`/`git reset` to exclude unrelated pre-existing working-tree changes (`docs/GAME-PROGRESS.md`, `src/lib/game/*`) → commit. No batch was committed with a red `check`.
- **Final state: all 133 units retrofitted, `bun run check` clean, 159 pages built.** Combined with the 5 units from the pilot/`build-vs-buy` sessions, every written unit in the project now has the `LevelIntro`/`Checkpoint` pattern applied — the rollout that began with "pilot this on 2 units" is now complete project-wide.
- Not done this session: no new content was written (this was purely a retrofit of existing units); `ROADMAP.md` statuses were untouched throughout (retrofitting a `done` unit doesn't change its status).
- Suggested next: with the retrofit rollout finished, the natural next session is back to normal unit authoring — pick a track/unit per the usual one-unit-per-session rule — or a deliberate pass to add `interactives.json` demos to any of the older units that predate rule 5c's "default to building one" guidance and don't yet have one.

- **Scenario throughline**: Renee (mid-level engineer, built the team's blameless-postmortem practice) and Jordan (staff engineer, ten years overall, three weeks on the team, writes Renee's calibration input this cycle) — a peer-mentoring case where formal seniority and domain seniority point opposite directions. L1 introduces the gap; L2 gives the four-move framing (ask, scope narrowly, credit what they bring, let them decide) plus the directive-vs-facilitative spectrum; L3 walks Renee's actual conversation with Jordan move-by-move, a "what if he'd been defensive" branch, and then a second, structurally different relationship — a formal skip-level pairing with Kai (an engineer two reporting levels below Renee's own skip-level manager) — to cover both halves of the roadmap problem statement (peer mentoring and skip-level relationships) with one coherent cast rather than two disconnected examples.

- `interactives.json`: one slider demo (`reception-vs-directiveness`) — three params (formal status gap, trust built, directiveness) producing a single output line (how well the approach is likely to land), directly modeling L2's "lean facilitative early; more directive gets safer as trust builds" point. Defaults (statusGap 7, trust 2, directiveness 3) are grounded in Renee and Jordan's actual numbers and reproduce a reception score of 65; dragging directiveness to its min/max moves the score to the correct 100/0 extremes.

## 2026-09-01 — career-craft/staff-scope, full L1-L3/exercises/interactives

Wrote `career-craft/staff-scope` end-to-end: "What does a staff engineer actually own that a senior doesn't? (staff scope: technical direction, org-level leverage)."

- **Scenario throughline**: Marcus Chen, a senior engineer on Payments at Northwind Commerce, up for staff this cycle, finds that three teams (Payments, Fulfillment, Search) have each hand-rolled their own non-idempotent retry logic against a flaky payment vendor, costing $40,000/quarter in duplicate-charge refunds org-wide. L1 poses the choice: ship a fast, correct fix for his own team by Friday (the senior-scope move), or spend the sprint building cross-team buy-in (the staff-scope move). L2 gives the framework — two currencies of leverage (individual execution vs. technical direction/org-level leverage), a three-question attention framework (blast radius, recurrence, systemic-fix leverage), and saying no as scope defense. L3 walks the whole quarter move by move: groundwork quantifying the problem, a design doc that folds in tech leads Devon Ruiz's and Sana Patel's real objections instead of dismissing them, sponsoring mid-level engineer Aliyah Osman to lead the actual build (rather than Marcus building it himself), and a negotiated no to VP Greg Whitfield's request for a sprint of unrelated prototyping work — closed with a decision worksheet and a question extending the framework to messier conditions (unrelated root causes, a peer request with no dollar figure to negotiate against).
- `LevelIntro`×3, `Checkpoint`×4 (2 in L2, 2 in L3), `Scenario`×2 (L1 and L3, each with a distinct `facts` slot — L1's is an icon list plus a progress bar showing the ~33% of the $40k Marcus recovers by only fixing his own team; L3's is a compact facts list naming the quarter's cast and one VP request).
- Non-technical/soft-skill track per rule 9: L3 is fully worked prose/tables/numbered-steps, no fake pseudocode-as-code blocks anywhere.
- Visual elements: 2 mermaid `flowchart`s (L1 senior-vs-staff scope, L2 two-currencies) plus 1 mermaid `xychart-beta` (L2, framework-question scoring) plus 1 more flowchart in L3 (the four-move sequence) — 4 diagrams total — plus 4 comparison/data tables spread across all three levels.
- `interactives.json`: one slider demo (`staff-scope-adoption-leverage`) — two params (teams adopting the shared library out of 6, and conformance rate) producing a single output line (monthly duplicate-charge cost avoided), back-derived from the unit's own $40k/quarter, 3-team baseline (~$4,444/team/month); kept to one output line per CLAUDE.md's no-arithmetic-complement guidance.
- `exercises.json`: 40 items (13/13/14 across L1/L2/L3), 16 pools (2-3 variants each, 6 pools in L1 including a `l1-friday-fix` pool added after an initial miscount left L1 at 11 items), all `quiz` type per rule 9 (career-craft is a soft-skill track — no code exercises). Every pool has a `reference` (icon list / compare box / mini table) and `learnMore` panel, verified identical across each pool's variants via a node script.
- Ran `bun run check` twice — first pass failed only on `format:check` (unformatted MDX tables), fixed with `bun run format`, second pass clean: 214 pages built, exit code 0.

## 2026-09-01 — career-craft/market-research, full L1-L3/exercises/interactives

Wrote `career-craft/market-research` end-to-end: "How do I evaluate whether an offer or raise is actually fair? (market research, total comp literacy)."

- **Scenario throughline**: Dana Okafor, senior backend engineer at Fernbank Robotics (private Series C), gets a raise letter offering $142,000 → $150,000 base (+5.6%) framed by her manager as generous — with no mention that her 4-year RSU grant is one tranche from a cliff to $0, while a competing Anchorline Systems offer looms. L1 poses the base-only-vs-total-comp trap; L2 gives the framework (total comp = base + bonus + equity; market bands and percentile-within-band; vesting schedules/refreshers/cliffs; current vs. grant equity valuation; multi-year trend over single-year snapshot); L3 walks Dana's actual research and negotiation — pulling real band data, building a multi-year total-comp table, and asking for a refresher grant instead of just a bigger base number.

- Visual elements: a `pie` chart of Dana's total comp breakdown (L1) plus additional mermaid diagrams/tables across L2/L3 illustrating the comp-evaluation framework and band/percentile concepts.
- `interactives.json`: one slider demo (`refresher-exit-value`) — refresher grant size and exit-value multiple vs. today's 409A price, producing Dana's next-year total comp, grounded in her actual $150k base/$15k bonus/$14-per-share numbers.
- `exercises.json`: 40 items (13/13/14 across L1/L2/L3), 18 pools, all `quiz` type per rule 9, verified via node script: 0 missing explanations, 0 non-quiz items, all pools' `reference`/`learnMore` identical across variants.

## 2026-09-01 — business-communication/visibility-self-promotion, full L1-L3/exercises/interactives

Wrote `business-communication/visibility-self-promotion` end-to-end: "How do I get credit for my work without appearing to seek it? (visibility without self-promotion)." First unit of a new pass through `business-communication`'s remaining 6 planned units.

- **Scenario throughline**: Priya, who quietly fixed a recurring checkout outage and posted a summary four people read, vs. Marcus, who shipped smaller work but mentioned it constantly to their manager Elena. L2 works out why "just do good work" fails (a manager can only advocate for what they can cite, not what they vaguely remember) and lays out concrete tactics — evidence-based visibility, credit-sharing, timing near decision points — that create visibility without reading as self-promotion. L3 walks Priya's next two quarters applying each tactic, plus the failure modes that turn each one back into invisibility or boastfulness if done wrong.

## 2026-09-03 — business-communication/upward-disagreement, full L1-L3/exercises/interactives

Wrote `business-communication/upward-disagreement` end-to-end: "How do I tell someone senior 'this plan will fail'? (upward disagreement, psychological safety)." Already scaffolded via `bun run new:unit business-communication upward-disagreement`; the three empty `.md` stubs were deleted and replaced with fresh `.mdx` content.

- **Scenario throughline**: Maya Torres, a senior backend engineer at Ledgerline (a payments infrastructure company), load-tests a new settlement ledger her VP, Marcus Chen, has already committed to the board will cut over in a single "big bang" weekend, two weeks before Black Friday — with no rollback path once it starts. L1 poses the problem and distinguishes upward disagreement from lateral pushback via the power gap and psychological safety. L2 works out the mechanism — why a public board commitment raises Marcus's face cost, the four moves (frame as inquiry, raise privately, bring evidence, lead with the shared goal) and what each one specifically prevents, and the decision tree for disagree-and-commit vs. escalating further. L3 walks Maya's actual private conversation with Marcus line by line, what she does when he proceeds anyway (disagree-and-commit, not silent resentment), when a second load test's new evidence justifies raising it again, and closes with two explicit extend-it questions (a public-room variant, an already-failing-in-production variant).

(translating technical risk to business risk)." Already scaffolded via `bun run new:unit business-communication translating-technical-risk-business-risk`; the three empty `.md` stubs were deleted and replaced with fresh `.mdx` content.

- **Scenario throughline**: Marcus, a staff engineer at an online retailer, explains to Elena (the COO) that the checkout database's connection pool is running at 62% utilization under normal load and could face "connection exhaustion under peak load" — all true, technically precise, and completely undecidable, so Elena replies "keep an eye on it" and nothing gets funded. L1 poses the gap and names the vocabulary (probability, impact, exposure window/"by when", catastrophizing, minimizing, options). L2 works out the mechanics: why raw technical severity doesn't move a business decision, the three-part translation (severity→probability+trend, "could cause an outage"→impact in dollars/customers, "urgent"→a specific date), how to avoid both catastrophizing and minimizing, and why the translation must end in at least two costed options rather than a single verdict. L3 walks Marcus and Elena's actual conversation statement by statement, diagnoses exactly why each accurate statement still gave Elena nothing to act on, then rewrites it — leading with the fact that Black-Friday-level (3x) traffic already exceeds capacity _today_, impact in dollars-per-minute-of-downtime, and a three-option cost/trade-off table — and closes with an extend-it question contrasting this capacity risk's growth-curve-driven timeline against a security vulnerability's disclosure/patch-driven timeline.
- `LevelIntro`×3, `Checkpoint`×4 (2 in L2, 2 in L3), `Scenario`×1 (L1, with a distinct `facts` slot: two utilization progress bars — 62% normal, 186%/over-capacity at Black-Friday-level traffic — plus the 8%/month growth-rate context).
- Non-technical/soft-skill track per rule 9: L3 is fully worked prose/dialogue/tables/numbered-options, no fake pseudocode-as-code anywhere, despite the subject matter being technical-risk-adjacent.
- Visual elements: 2 mermaid diagrams (L2's `flowchart` on why a technical fact does/doesn't answer "what does this cost, by when," and L2's `xychart-beta` projecting checkout DB utilization crossing 100% around week 11) plus 6 comparison/vocabulary/options tables spread across all three levels (L1 vocabulary table, L2's technical→business framing table, L2's catastrophize/minimize/calibrated table, L3's options-with-cost table, plus supporting tables).
- `interactives.json`: one slider demo (`months-until-capacity-exceeded`) — current DB utilization, monthly traffic growth rate, and a peak-event traffic multiplier (Black Friday ≈ 3x) vs. months until capacity is exceeded, grounded in and consistent with L2/L3's 62%/8%/3x numbers; kept to one output line per CLAUDE.md's no-arithmetic-complement guidance. At default params (62%, 8%/mo, 1x) it shows ~6 months of runway; dragging the peak multiplier to 3x drops it to 0 — capacity is already exceeded today at Black-Friday-level traffic, matching the unit's own narrative.

## 2026-09-03 — software-design/classic-design-patterns, full L1-L3/exercises/interactives

Wrote `software-design/classic-design-patterns` end-to-end: "How do I recognize a design pattern I actually need vs. cargo-culting one? (classic design patterns, when they help vs. hurt)."

- **Scenario throughline**: Sam ships a 2-branch (domestic/international) shipping-cost if/else at Widgetly; a senior reviewer, Priya, demands a Strategy-pattern rewrite as "best practice." L1 poses the actual question (does the code have a problem the pattern fixes, or is this résumé-driven design) and introduces the Gang-of-Four catalog's three families (creational/structural/behavioral) plus cargo-culting and résumé-driven design as the named failure modes. L2 builds a 4-question checklist (real concrete variants today? do they vary independently? is the direct version already costing something? does the indirection buy something concrete?) and a 5-pattern catalog sketch (Strategy, Factory Method, Decorator, Observer, Singleton) with problem/cost/good-sign/bad-sign columns, then runs the checklist side-by-side against Sam's shipping calculator (skip the pattern) and a second scenario, payment processing with 5+ processors added independently by different teams (use the pattern) — same pattern, opposite verdict, driven entirely by the code's actual shape. L3 works both scenarios in real, runnable TypeScript: the over-applied Strategy version of the shipping calculator next to its right-sized direct version, then the payment-processing case where Strategy earns its cost, plus Decorator/Factory/Observer/Adapter doing real work and a look at a pattern that was right when adopted but outlived its reason.
- `LevelIntro`×3, `Checkpoint`×4 (2 in L2, 2 in L3, confirmed by grep), `Scenario`×1 (L1, with a distinct `facts` slot: current-code/review-comment/proposed-change/open-question fact rows, icon-prefixed).

- Visual elements: 1 mermaid flowchart (L2, the 4-question checklist as a decision tree) plus 4 comparison/catalog tables across L1/L2 (pattern-family table, cost-side table, 5-pattern catalog table, checklist-applied-to-both-scenarios table).
- `interactives.json`: one slider demo (`pattern-cost-crossover`) modeling direct-if/else cost (grows with a coupling penalty per pair of branches) vs. Strategy-pattern cost (fixed upfront + flat per variant) against number of variants — grounded in the unit's own two scenarios: defaults (2 variants, 0.3 penalty) reproduce Sam's shipping calculator where direct code is cheaper (2.6 vs. 6), dragging to 5 variants reproduces the payment-processing case where the lines cross and the pattern becomes cheaper (11 vs. 9). Single crossover relationship, not two arithmetic-complement lines.

## 2026-09-03 — software-design/refactoring-technique, full L1-L3/exercises/interactives

Wrote `software-design/refactoring-technique` end-to-end: "How do I change a design safely once it's already tangled? (refactoring technique, safe transformation steps)." The three `.md` stubs were replaced with fresh `.mdx` content (each now imports `Scenario`/`LevelIntro`/`Checkpoint`).

- **Scenario throughline**: Dana, a backend engineer at Boxtree (a mail-order retailer), has to add a loyalty-tier discount to `calculateOrderTotal` — a 68-line function with 4 levels of nesting, magic numbers throughout, and the same "is this a real member" check written three different ways in three places (one of which is missing a condition the other two have — a real, currently-live latent bug hiding inside the duplication). L1 draws the refactoring-vs-rewriting line and reads the function's smells (duplicated logic, long function, deep nesting, magic numbers) via a comparison table. L2 gives the safe-refactor loop (small step → tests → commit or revert) as a mermaid flowchart, a 7-move catalog mapped to the smell each one fixes, and works out why a refactor and a behavior change can never share a commit. L3 is the full worked example in plain, zero-dependency Node.js: six characterization tests written first (including one that documents the duplication bug's actual current — quirky — behavior, on purpose), then four small behavior-preserving refactoring commits shown as real diffs (Extract Variable, Extract Function, Remove Duplication, Guard Clauses + Lookup Table), each independently re-verified against the same unchanged test suite, followed by one more preparatory extraction and finally the loyalty-tier feature landing as its own deliberate, behavior-changing commit with its own new test. Closes with a step-by-step summary table and two extend-it questions (a function with zero starting tests; a seventh shipping region).
- `LevelIntro`×3, `Checkpoint`×4 (2 in L2, 2 in L3, confirmed by grep), `Scenario`×2 (L1 and L3, each with a distinct `facts` slot — L1's is a function-health readout (length bar, nesting depth, duplication count, test coverage) via icon rows and a progress bar; L3's is a compact inputs/responsibilities/tests/quirk recap).
- Technical track per rule 9: all L3 code is real, runnable, zero-dependency JavaScript (`node order-pricing.js` / `node test-order-pricing.js`, no framework or build step) — chosen deliberately so every step is immediately, individually runnable, matching the unit's own point about per-step verifiability. All arithmetic in the worked example (6 characterization-test totals plus the capped-discount feature test) was independently recomputed and verified against actual `node -e` output before being written into the content.
- Visual elements: 1 mermaid flowchart (L2, the safe-refactor loop) plus 5 tables spread across all three levels (L1: refactor-vs-rewrite comparison, smell/symptom/cost table; L2: move-to-smell catalog table; L3: step-by-step move/smell/behavior-change summary table, trade-offs/failure-modes table).
- `interactives.json`: one slider demo (`batch-size-vs-undetected-risk`) — number of structural steps bundled into one commit before testing, and each step's own error rate, vs. the compounding risk that an undetected behavior slip survives — grounded in the unit's own six-step example: at the discipline's default (1 step per commit, 5% per-step error rate) risk is 5%; dragging batch size to 6 (bundling all of Boxtree's six steps into one commit) reproduces roughly 26.5%, verified against `node -e` computation before writing the JSON. Single output line, no arithmetic-complement pairing.
- `exercises.json`: 40 items (13/13/14 across L1/L2/L3), 15 pools (2-3 variants each, 5 pools per level), quiz for L1/L2 (26 items) and code for L3 (14 items) per rule 9's technical-track split. Every code exercise's expected test values (including floating-point sums like `0.1 + 0.05`) was checked against actual `node -e` output, not hand-computed, before being written in. Verified via a Python structural script: 40/40 items present, level split exactly 13/13/14, 15 pools all internally consistent (single level, single type, identical `reference`/`learnMore` across variants), 0 missing explanations, 0 code items missing `solution`, 0 duplicate ids.

## 2026-09-03 — software-design/technical-debt-deliberate-trade-off, full L1-L3/exercises/interactives

Wrote `software-design/technical-debt-deliberate-trade-off` end-to-end: "How do I know how much technical debt is acceptable? (technical debt as a deliberate trade-off, not just a sin)." The three `.md` stubs were replaced with fresh `.mdx` content (each now imports `Scenario`/`LevelIntro`/`Checkpoint`).

- **Scenario throughline**: Nadia, a backend engineer at Lumeo (a three-person project-tracking SaaS startup), has three weeks to launch for one paying customer, Anchorline Logistics. Building the data layer the proper, tenant-scoped way is ~~15 engineer-days (~~$9,000 loaded); hardcoding a single-tenant assumption is 2 days — and multi-tenancy is genuinely needed in ~2 quarters when two more contracts close. L1 introduces the loan metaphor (principal/interest/interest rate/default) via a comparison table and draws the line between real debt (something was gained) and plain poor quality (nothing was). L2 builds the debt quadrant (deliberate/inadvertent × prudent/reckless) as a styled 2×2 grid, classifying Nadia's shortcut correctly only when it's logged with a repayment plan, then sizes the trade-off as interest-per-sprint ($1,200) vs. principal ($9,000) via a mermaid flowchart. L3 works the full numeric case: real before/after `data.js` diffs (single-tenant vs. tenant-scoped, including the caller fan-out that's where the 15 days actually goes), a zero-dependency `debt-interest.js` calculator computing the exact break-even sprint (7.5), an `xychart-beta` chart showing cumulative interest crossing the flat principal line, a debt-log/register example, and failure modes (debt never made visible, debt without leadership buy-in, "debt" that's actually just poor quality, principal that grows as more code depends on the shortcut). Closes with an extend-it question: does the calculus change if the next contract requires per-tenant data residency from day one.
- `LevelIntro`×3, `Checkpoint`×4 (2 in L2, 2 in L3, confirmed by grep), `Scenario`×2 (L1 and L3, each with a distinct `facts` slot — L1's is a launch-deadline/cost-bar/multi-tenancy-date/interest-per-sprint icon list, L3's is a principal/interest/break-even/forced-deadline recap).

Every dollar figure in the prose (principal $9,000, interest $1,200/sprint, break-even sprint 7.5, cumulative-interest-at-sprint-13 $15,600, overpay-by-waiting $6,600) was independently recomputed via `node -e` before being written in, and the `xychart-beta` chart's two data series were cross-checked against the same numbers.
- Visual elements: 2 mermaid diagrams (L2's flowchart on sizing/repaying a shortcut, L3's `xychart-beta` crossing cumulative-interest-vs-principal lines) plus a styled 2×2 HTML quadrant grid (L2) and 9 tables spread across all three levels (L1: loan-term table, real-debt-vs-bad-code framing; L3: debt-log table, step numbers embedded in the trade-offs prose).

## 2026-09-03 — software-design/api-design, full L1-L3/exercises/interactives

Wrote `software-design/api-design` end-to-end: "How do I design an interface or API that's hard to misuse? (API design, defensive design, making illegal states unrepresentable)." The three empty `.md` stubs (already scaffolded, `ROADMAP.md` row already `in-progress`) were deleted and replaced with fresh `.mdx` content.

- **Scenario throughline**: Maya, on-call at Alertly (a company whose product is paging humans during incidents), doesn't get paged during a real 3 a.m. payments outage because the internal `sendAlert(recipientId, message, channel, isUrgent, emailAddress?, phoneNumber?, deviceToken?)` signature let an escalation call pass `channel: "sms"` with `deviceToken` set but `phoneNumber` left `undefined` — a nonsensical combination the signature happily accepted. A runtime check for this existed, but lived deep inside the SMS-client code, inside a `try/catch` written for a different purpose (swallowing flaky-provider errors), so the missing-field error got silently absorbed and logged at `debug`. L1 poses the "whose job was it — the caller's or the function's?" question and names the vocabulary (illegal states unrepresentable, parse-don't-validate, fail fast, minimal surface area, least astonishment, boolean/stringly-typed traps). L2 diagnoses exactly why the existing runtime check was powerless (its _placement_, not its logic) and builds the parse-at-the-boundary mental model plus a discriminated-union mechanics section (how a tagged shape structurally removes a field slot rather than just adding a check). L3 rebuilds `sendAlert()` fully in real TypeScript: the permissive v1 diagnosed field-by-field, a redesigned `AlertChannel`/`Urgency` discriminated union plus a `parseAlertRequest`/`parseChannel` parsing boundary, a `// @ts-expect-error` demonstration that Maya's exact bug no longer compiles, real Vitest-style tests, a trade-offs table (quick internal script vs. public library vs. open-ended plugin channels vs. a union that grows to 12 kinds), a concrete migration-adapter pattern for tightening a live API without breaking existing callers, and an extend-it question about modeling "all three channels at once, on purpose" as a legitimate option instead of a bug.
- `LevelIntro`×3, `Checkpoint`×3 (1 in L2, 2 in L3, confirmed by grep), `Scenario`×1 (L1, with a distinct `facts` slot: an icon-row recap of the API signature, the exact call that failed, the silent failure mode, and the 45-minute cost — not a copy of the narrative prose).
- Technical track per rule 9: all L3 code is real TypeScript (discriminated unions, exhaustive `switch` narrowing, a parsing boundary, a migration adapter, Vitest tests) — TypeScript chosen explicitly (stated in-text) for consistency with the rest of the site and because its structural union types make "illegal states unrepresentable" directly demonstrable via a genuine compile error, not just a runtime check with better wording.
- Visual elements: 1 mermaid flowchart (L2, contrasting where an invalid combination gets caught — compile time / runtime-if-reached / production-silent) plus 4 tables spread across all three levels (L1: where-the-check-lives comparison; L2: defensive-design-moves checklist, discriminated-union field-slot table; L3: trade-offs/failure-modes table).
- `interactives.json`: one slider demo (`misuse-surface-explosion`) — number of optional/boolean fields on a permissive API (2^n combinations, exponential) vs. number of valid states in the safe discriminated-union redesign (a small, independent, roughly-flat quantity) — grounded in Alertly's own shape (defaults: 4 flags ≈ `emailAddress`/`phoneNumber`/`deviceToken`/`isUrgent`, 3 valid channels ≈ email/sms/push), verified via a Python script that defaults fall within param bounds and `compute` runs correctly (16 vs. 3 at default). Two genuinely independent quantities, not an arithmetic-complement pair.
- `exercises.json`: 40 items (13/13/14 across L1/L2/L3), 18 pools (2-3 variants each, 6 pools per level), quiz for L1/L2 (26 items) and code for L3 (14 items) per rule 9's technical-track split. Every code exercise's `solution` was executed against its own `tests[].expr` strings in a Node harness replicating the sandboxed Worker's `expect().toBe/toEqual/toBeTruthy/toThrow` API (script written and deleted this session) — 14/14 code exercises pass, 0 failures. Verified via a Python structural script: 40/40 items present, level split exactly 13/13/14, 18 pools all internally consistent (single level, single type, identical `reference`/`learnMore` across variants), 0 missing explanations, 0 code items missing `solution`, 0 duplicate ids.

## 2026-09-03 — software-design/evolutionary-design, full L1-L3/exercises/interactives

Wrote `software-design/evolutionary-design` end-to-end: "How do I evolve a design as requirements change without a rewrite? (evolutionary design, the open-closed principle in practice)." The three empty `.md` stubs (already scaffolded, `ROADMAP.md` row already `in-progress`) were deleted and replaced with fresh `.mdx` content.

- **Scenario throughline**: Fernbridge, a small invoicing SaaS, evolves its pricing engine through three real rounds of requirements. Round 0 is a 6-line baseline (subtotal + 8% tax). Round 1 adds a single volume-discount `if` — deliberately left as a plain conditional, since one instance is correctly not worth a seam yet (YAGNI wins). Round 2 brings a second, independent requirement (a coupon code) plus a processing-fee waiver close together, and the naive "just add another if" approach ships a real, demonstrated order-of-operations bug — the coupon branch reads from the original `subtotal` instead of the already-discounted `running` total, silently erasing the volume discount whenever a coupon is present. This is the concrete second-variant signal that justifies extracting a `PricingRule` interface + ordered list (the open-closed seam), fixing the bug structurally rather than patching the one bad line. Round 3 brings two more requirements, independently: a VIP loyalty discount (same axis — appends cleanly to the existing `PricingRule` list, zero lines of existing code touched) and a region-based tax rate (a genuinely different axis — tax computation, not a pre-tax adjustment — so it gets its own small `TaxRateResolver` seam rather than being forced through `PricingRule`). L3 closes with a trade-offs/failure-modes table (guessing the wrong axis, reusing the wrong seam, a seam nobody ever uses twice, too-late extraction) and an extend-it question about whether a round-4 subscription-billing requirement would fit the existing seams or demand a third one.

## 2026-09-03 — software-design/long-term-design-judgment, full L1-L3/exercises/interactives

Wrote `software-design/long-term-design-judgment` end-to-end: "How does a staff engineer make a design decision that will outlive their own tenure on the project? (long-term design judgment, designing for whoever inherits it)." The three empty `.md` stubs (already scaffolded, `ROADMAP.md` row already `in-progress`) were deleted and replaced with fresh `.mdx` content. **This is the last unit in the `software-design/` track — all 13 units are now `done`.**

- **Scenario throughline**: Renata, a staff engineer at Northlane (a project-tracking SaaS), has to design how the product checks feature entitlements (plan tier, add-ons, grandfathered contract exceptions) — and she's rotating onto a different team in 6 months, so she won't be the one explaining the design when someone questions it later. She has two working options: a clever, config-driven rule engine with its own hand-rolled boolean-expression DSL (fast to extend without a deploy, but understood fluently by only its author), and a plain ordered array of named `EntitlementRule` TypeScript objects (more boilerplate per rule, but readable by anyone on the team with zero prior context). L1 poses the framing question and introduces the vocabulary (legibility, cleverness, ADR, one-way/two-way door, lock-in). L2 builds out why "it works" isn't a sufficient bar for a decision meant to outlive its author, the legibility-vs-cleverness trade (who benefits from each), why code alone never preserves rejected-alternative reasoning, the one-way/two-way door lens for calibrating how much caution a decision deserves, and why lock-in's real cost arrives on a delay (a two-way door calcifying into a one-way one as dependents accrue). L3 works the full decision in real TypeScript (both interfaces built out, including Vitest tests for the plain version), a complete worked ADR-014 document as a durable artifact (context/decision/rejected-alternatives/consequences, including an explicit boundary naming a related-but-separate ADR-015), a failure-modes table (over-applying "legible over clever," a one-way door disguised as two-way, an ADR written and never read, designing for a future reader who never appears), and two extend-it questions (team scaling to 40 engineers across 6 teams; the DSL having already shipped for 3 months with real dependents before the ADR was raised).

- **Scenario throughline**: Mira, a backend engineer at Kelvin Metrics (a small monitoring SaaS), hardcodes a production database password into `config/database.js` under deadline pressure ("just for this branch"), pushes it to a repo whose visibility had been accidentally flipped public. This unit's actual territory is the trust boundary of third-party code you didn't write: transitive dependencies nobody explicitly chose, real attack patterns (maintainer takeover, typosquatting, dependency confusion, compromised CI/build pipelines), and the lockfile/SBOM/scanning/pinning defense stack.
- **Scenario throughline**: Priya, a platform engineer at Northfield Ledger (a small invoicing SaaS), discovers CI has been leaking AWS credentials for 14 days via `case-fold`, a small formatting utility pulled in as a transitive dependency of `ledger-pdf` (a library Northfield chose deliberately) — never reviewed directly by anyone at Northfield. The incident follows a documented real-world pattern (maintainer takeover, modeled loosely on the `event-stream`-style 2018 npm incidents, without claiming to report exact unverified facts): a burned-out solo maintainer hands off publish access, and the new maintainer ships a routine-looking patch bump with an added `postinstall` script that scrapes CI's environment variables. `npm audit` stayed clean throughout, since no CVE existed for a brand-new, never-before-reported malicious version. L1 poses "if nobody added it and nothing was vulnerable, how did it end up running arbitrary code" and names the four attack patterns and four-part defense stack. L2 builds the transitive-trust mental model (why a PR reviewing `ledger-pdf`'s bump never surfaces `case-fold`'s new range two hops down) and the lockfile-integrity/SBOM/scanning architecture as one continuous pipeline with a feedback loop (new CVEs re-scanned against old SBOMs). L3 makes every defense real and runnable in plain Node.js: an integrity-hash verifier, a dependency-tree walker flagging loose (`^`/`~`) ranges vs. exact pins, a minimal CycloneDX-shaped SBOM generator, a worked end-to-end SBOM scan against a stand-in advisory database that actually flags `case-fold@2.4.1`, a Levenshtein-distance typosquat detector (deliberately shown to miss `case-fold` itself, since it's the real name under real publish rights — a different attack pattern), and a GitHub Actions CI step using `--ignore-scripts` to verify before any install script gets to run.
- `LevelIntro`×3, `Checkpoint`×4 (2 in L2 — why `npm audit` didn't catch a brand-new malicious version, and what an SBOM lets a team answer in minutes vs. days; 2 in L3 — whether exact pinning alone would have prevented the incident, and whether the typosquat detector would have caught it), `Scenario`×1 (L1 only, per the pattern used when L2/L3 open with a bridging sentence into a guiding question instead — see `secrets-management`/`authentication-fundamentals`), with a distinct `facts` slot (a 5-point timeline from the package's download count down to detection, plus a "nobody at Northfield ever chose case-fold" callout, not a copy of the narrative prose).
- Technical track per rule 9: all L3 code is real, runnable, framework-free Node.js (integrity checker via a self-contained hash stand-in since the exercise sandbox has no `node:crypto`, tree walker, SBOM generator/scanner, typosquat detector) plus real GitHub Actions YAML for the CI wiring — matching the site's existing `security/` units' plain-JS style.
- Visual elements: 2 mermaid flowcharts (L2 — the transitive-trust path from `package.json` through `ledger-pdf` to `case-fold` to exfiltration, and the SBOM-generate/scan/re-scan pipeline with its feedback loop), 3 tables across L1–L2 (L1: dependency-layer-to-chooser table, defense-stack-to-protection table; L2: none additional — the L2 tables are inline in the lockfile/SBOM sections), plus icon-row grids in L1 for the four attack patterns and the four-part defense stack.
