## 2026-09-04 — security/defense-depth, full L1-L3/exercises/interactives

Wrote `security/defense-depth` end-to-end: "How do we design a system assuming a component will be breached? (defense in depth, least privilege, zero trust)." The three empty `.md` stubs (already scaffolded by `new:unit`, `ROADMAP.md` row already `in-progress`) were deleted and replaced with fresh `.mdx` content. This is unit 10 of 13 in the `security/` track — 9 are now `done`, 3 remain `planned` (`security-testing`, `incident-response`, `secure-by-design`).

- **Scope check against sibling units** (read each sibling's L1 first, per the task): confirmed this unit stays out of `security-mindset`'s general threat-modeling posture, `authentication-fundamentals`'s identity-proving mechanics, `authorization-models`'s single-request RBAC/ABAC check, `hashing`'/`symmetric-asymmetric-encryption-basics`'/`tls-https`'s specific crypto mechanisms, `owasp-top-10`'s vulnerability-category survey, `secrets-management`'s credential-leakage lifecycle, and `supply-chain-security`'s third-party-dependency trust. This unit's actual territory is the architectural synthesis those units don't cover: assuming any ONE of those defenses eventually fails, what stops that single failure from becoming total compromise — defense in depth as independent layered controls, least privilege as blast-radius containment, zero trust as removing network location as an implicit trust signal, and the real cost/benefit trade-off of where to spend the defense budget.
- **Scenario throughline**: Meridian Retail and Castellan Retail are breached through the identical 0-day (an image-resizing library) on identically-patched web servers, on the same weekend. Meridian's web server holds full admin DB credentials with a direct network route to the database ("just give it admin, it's simpler") — 14,000,000 customer records exfiltrated. Castellan's identical foothold hits a wall: network segmentation with no direct route to the database except through a proxy, and the one account that proxy uses is scoped to `SELECT` on 2 tables (products: 8,200 rows, product_categories: 340 rows) — 8,540 non-sensitive records read, no lateral movement possible. L1 poses "both companies got breached, only one had to notify 14M customers — what was actually different, given the vulnerability itself was identical?" L2 builds the layered-controls model (a 7-layer mermaid flowchart from network segmentation through monitoring, each layer assuming the one before it already failed), the least-privilege blast-radius table, and a perimeter-only-vs-zero-trust comparison diagram. L3 makes every layer real and runnable: actual `GRANT`/`REVOKE`/row-level-security SQL for the scoped DB account, a 3-policy Kubernetes `NetworkPolicy` YAML set (default-deny-all + web-tier-egress + db-tier-ingress) enforcing L2's segmentation diagram, a TypeScript `authorizeQuery()` zero-trust per-request authorization check keyed off a verified mTLS cert CN (not a spoofable header) against an explicit `SERVICE_PERMISSIONS` table, and a `blastRadius()` calculator that reproduces Meridian's 14,000,000 vs. Castellan's 8,540 as a computed number over each company's own actual grants rather than a narrative claim.
- `LevelIntro`×3, `Checkpoint`×4 (2 in L2 — whether a second same-vendor-category WAF counts as a real second layer, and whether mTLS + per-request authz on an internal-only call is redundant given the caller can't be reached externally anyway; 2 in L3 — whether NetworkPolicy alone without the scoped DB account would still have contained the breach, and the blast-radius calculator's real limitation of only measuring direct grants, not multi-hop lateral movement), `Scenario`×1 (L1 only, per the pattern used when L2/L3 open with a bridging sentence into a guiding question instead — see `secrets-management`/`supply-chain-security`), with a distinct `facts` slot (the shared 0-day foothold plus a 2x2 Meridian-vs-Castellan outcome grid, not a copy of the narrative prose).
- Technical track per rule 9: all L3 code/config is real and runnable — PostgreSQL SQL for the GRANT/REVOKE/row-level-security policy, real Kubernetes `NetworkPolicy` YAML, TypeScript for the mTLS-keyed authorization check and the blast-radius calculator, matching the site's existing `security/` units' plain-code style.

- **Scope check against sibling units** (read each sibling's L1 first, per the task, plus `testing-quality/`'s L1s to keep general test-methodology content out): confirmed this unit stays out of `security-mindset`'s general threat-modeling posture, `owasp-top-10`'s vulnerability catalog (referenced briefly, not re-taught), `authentication-fundamentals`/`authorization-models`/`hashing`/`symmetric-asymmetric-encryption-basics`/`tls-https`'s specific mechanisms (things being tested, not the testing practice), `secrets-management`'s credential-leakage lifecycle (one finding category a scanner might catch, not the discipline), `supply-chain-security`'s deep SBOM/dependency-trust dive (SCA here stays to the automated-scan step only), and `defense-depth`'s architectural posture. This unit's actual territory is the verification practice itself: SAST, DAST, SCA, fuzzing, and manual pentesting — what each catches, what each structurally misses, where each sits in a CI/CD pipeline, and why automated tooling alone is never sufficient.
- **Scenario throughline**: `checkout-service-aegis` and `checkout-service-vantage`, two teams adding an identical search endpoint with string-concatenated SQL the same week. Aegis's pull request never merges — a SAST scanner wired into CI flags the exact line, fixed in the same PR. Vantage has no scanner; the identical bug ships to production and is exploited 19 days later. Six months later, Aegis gets breached anyway — not through anything a scanner could flag, but a pentest finding that setting order quantity negative pays the customer at checkout instead of charging them. L1 poses "if Aegis had every automated scanner running, why did they still need someone to try to break the product on purpose?" and names the five techniques. L2 builds the shift-left pipeline (commit→SAST, build→SCA, staging→DAST, continuous→fuzzing on parsers, periodic→pentest, continuous→bug bounty) as one continuous cost/speed/depth trade-off, plus the SAST/DAST/SCA/pentest comparison table. L3 makes every automated layer real and runnable in plain Node.js: a SAST-style regex rule engine (SQL-concat, `eval()`, hardcoded-secret rules) run against Aegis's actual PR diff plus its own false-positive/false-negative demonstration, a fuzz harness finding real crashes in a hand-rolled query-string parser, a DAST-style header/error prober against a mock server, a GitHub Actions CI gate wiring SAST+SCA with a severity threshold, and a full pentest-engagement table (scope, rules of engagement, CVSS severity, remediation SLA, retest) mapped back to Aegis's own critical-severity refund finding.
- `LevelIntro`×3, `Checkpoint`×4 (2 in L2 — which technique/stage catches dependency CVEs, and whether a clean DAST scan across six months means the app has no exploitable request path; 2 in L3 — whether a SAST false positive on a safe concatenation is a scanner bug, and why a critical refund bug and a medium clickjacking header get different remediation SLAs despite both being real findings), `Scenario`×1 (L1 only, per the pattern used when L2/L3 open with a bridging sentence into a guiding question instead — see `defense-depth`/`supply-chain-security`), with a distinct `facts` slot (the Aegis-vs-Vantage outcome grid plus the six-months-later pentest callout, not a copy of the narrative prose).

## 2026-09-04 — security/incident-response, full L1-L3/exercises/interactives

Wrote `security/incident-response` end-to-end: "How do we respond when a breach actually happens? (incident response, disclosure, post-mortems)." The three empty `.md` stubs (already scaffolded by `new:unit`, `ROADMAP.md` row already `in-progress`) were deleted and replaced with fresh `.mdx` content. This is unit 12 of 13 in the `security/` track — 12 are now `done`, 1 remains `planned` (`secure-by-design`).

- **Scope check against sibling units** (read each sibling's L1 first, per the task, plus a grep across `sustainable-performance/` and `people-management/` for any existing general incident-response/post-mortem content): confirmed this unit stays out of every prevention/detection-focused sibling (`security-mindset`, `authentication-fundamentals`, `authorization-models`, `hashing`, `symmetric-asymmetric-encryption-basics`, `tls-https`, `owasp-top-10`, `secrets-management`, `supply-chain-security`, `defense-depth`, `security-testing`) — all of those stop before or during an exploit attempt, while this unit starts from the moment prevention has already failed. Confirmed no generic (non-security) incident-response/post-mortem unit exists elsewhere: `systems/timeouts` and `systems/redundancy` are about resilience mechanisms (retries, failover), and `sustainable-performance/recovery-practices-crunch` is about personal recovery after burnout/incidents, not a security-specific IR discipline — so this unit stays the site's one true security incident-response unit, grounded in breach containment, forensics, and disclosure obligations rather than re-teaching generic on-call practice.
- **Scenario throughline**: Dana, an on-call SRE at Northlake Benefits (HR/benefits SaaS with EU customers, so GDPR applies), is paged at 2:14am for an anomalous database read-replica load. She finds `svc-enrollment-export` — an over-scoped admin service account — has been quietly exfiltrating 300–410 records nightly for 11 nights via a reused, externally-compromised credential. L1 poses the fork itself: isolate the session immediately (stops the bleeding, may lose volatile evidence or tip off the attacker) vs. watch it a while longer (clean forensic picture, but exfiltration continues) — a decision she has to make alone, before legal or exec even know a breach might exist. L2 builds the six-phase NIST-style lifecycle (mermaid flowchart), a concrete containment decision tree (mermaid), and the three-audiences/three-clocks disclosure model (internal → regulator → customer), including why GDPR's 72-hour clock starts at _awareness_, not at when the breach began. L3 makes the whole incident real and runnable: a full timestamped incident log table, a Node.js log-reconstruction pipeline (`parseLogLines`/`groupIntoSessions`/`flagAnomalousSessions`/`reconstructAttackerTimeline`) run against redacted real-shaped log lines that correctly separates the attacker's session from routine nightly exports, a chain-of-custody hash-sealing pair (`sealEvidence`/`verifyEvidenceIntact`), a 7-step containment runbook artifact, and a fully filled-in blameless post-mortem document (summary/timeline/root cause/contributing factors/action items) for the Northlake incident, deliberately naming a system gap (the account's excess scope) rather than a person.
- `LevelIntro`×3, `Checkpoint`×4 (2 in L2 — applying the containment decision tree to a tamper-evident-logged, non-active foothold, and confirming exactly when the GDPR clock starts relative to the 11-day dwell time; 2 in L3 — the anomaly-detection rule's structural blind spot to trusted-IP/business-hours exfiltration, and why naming an individual in the post-mortem "for accountability" undermines rather than helps it), `Scenario`×1 (L1 only, per the pattern used when L2/L3 open with a bridging sentence into a guiding question instead — see `defense-depth`/`security-testing`), with a distinct `facts` slot (the 2am page → 11-day-dwell discovery → containment-vs-preservation fork, plus a red/amber outcome comparison, not a copy of the narrative prose).
- Technical track per rule 9: all L3 code is real, runnable, framework-free JavaScript with no Node-specific APIs beyond what the sandboxed exercise Worker supports (`Date`, `JSON.parse`, plain arithmetic) — the log-reconstruction pipeline, the chain-of-custody hash sealing (illustrated with `node:crypto` in the deep-dive prose since that page isn't sandboxed, but every corresponding _code exercise_ was deliberately written without it to stay Worker-compatible), and the containment-classification function. ## 2026-09-04 — product-domain/translating-capability-into-their-vocabulary-cfo, full L1-L3/exercises/interactives — first unit of the product-domain track

Wrote `product-domain/translating-capability-into-their-vocabulary-cfo` end-to-end: "How do I speak the language of the person I'm selling to? (translating capability into their vocabulary — a CFO hears cost, ops hears reliability, a founder hears speed)." The three empty `.md` stubs (already scaffolded by `new:unit`, `ROADMAP.md` row already `in-progress`) were deleted and replaced with fresh `.mdx` content. This is the **first unit written in the `product-domain` track** (row 08 of 13; the second of the 7 remaining tracks per `docs/HANDOFF.md`, after `security` completed the previous session).

- **Scope check against sibling units** (read each sibling's `L1-summary` first): confirmed this unit stays distinct from `understanding-user-s-problem-solution` (understanding the problem before building), `requirements-gathering` (turning vague asks into requirements), `domain-driven-design-basics`/`bounded-contexts` (modeling business domains in code/architecture), and `validation` (validating you're building the right thing) — none of those touch stakeholder-specific pitch vocabulary. Most importantly, this unit builds directly on `value-framing-over-feature-framing` (the general "lead with value, not features" principle for one listener) but goes further: it's the specific skill of recognizing that _different_ stakeholders in the _same_ deal hear the same capability differently, and translating it three separate ways within one sale — audience-segmentation within a single deal, not audience-awareness in the abstract. That unit is referenced briefly (a dedicated "How this differs from leading with value" section in L1, plus an exercise pool on the relationship) rather than re-taught. Also checked `buyer-psychology` (the psychology behind an objection that's already surfaced) — confirmed this unit is upstream/parallel: correct per-stakeholder framing _before_ an objection arises, not handling one that already has.
- **Scenario throughline**: a vendor pitches the same order-error-catching software to a CFO, an ops lead, and a founder together in one room, using one generic framing ("a proactive quality-assurance platform") — polite non-committal response, no follow-up. A second meeting translates the identical capability three ways (CFO: ~$288k/year in prevented error costs, payback under two months; ops lead: one config change, no new on-call load, a false flag never halts the whole pipeline; founder: removes a manual step currently costing two days per release cycle) — a yes. L2 builds the stakeholder map (accountability → vocabulary → translated capability, as both a table and a one-capability-branching-into-three mermaid diagram), the three-step translation method, and concrete discovery tactics for finding out who's actually in the room before pitching. L3 (per rule 9 — `product-domain` is non-technical/soft-skill, so no fake code) walks the full worked pitch to each stakeholder in sequence with the actual language used, a wrong-vocabulary contrast per stakeholder (what the CFO pitch sounds like wasted on the ops lead, etc.), the combined-room sequencing version, and an extension section per rule 4b ("what changes if..." — a stakeholder holding two accountabilities at once, and honest framing when the news is worse, e.g. a 14-month payback instead of 2).
- `LevelIntro`×3, `Checkpoint`×4 (2 in L2 — whether a dollar figure quoted to an ops lead counts as translated into their vocabulary, and the best first move when a meeting's stakeholder makeup is unknown; 2 in L3 — whether the "more impressive" number is always the safer pick regardless of listener, and whether the order translations are spoken in matters), `Scenario`×1 (L1 only, per the pattern used when L2/L3 open with a bridging sentence into a guiding question instead), with a distinct `facts` slot (an icon-per-stakeholder list of what each one heard, and the polite-no vs. yes outcome, not a copy of the narrative prose).

- `interactives.json`: one slider demo (`matched-vocabularies-vs-close-rate`, L2) — a single 0–3 param (how many of the three stakeholders in the room got a correctly matched translation) driving an estimated close-rate percentage (15% baseline, +22 points per correctly matched stakeholder, capped at 95%), grounded in the unit's own two-meeting scenario (0 matched → lukewarm, 3 matched → yes); single output line per the no-arithmetic-complement-pairing rule.

## 2026-09-04 — product-domain/value-based-selling, full L1-L3/exercises/interactives

Wrote `product-domain/value-based-selling` end-to-end: "How do I turn 'that's technically impressive' into 'I want to pay for this'? (value-based selling, ROI framing, appreciation vs. comprehension)." The three empty `.md` stubs (already scaffolded by `new:unit`, `ROADMAP.md` row already `in-progress`) were deleted and replaced with fresh `.mdx` content.

- **Scope check against sibling units** (read each sibling's `L1-summary` first): confirmed this unit stays distinct from `understanding-user-s-problem-solution`, `requirements-gathering`, `domain-driven-design-basics`/`bounded-contexts`, and `validation` (none touch pitch/ROI mechanics). Builds directly on `value-framing-over-feature-framing` (value over features, in general) and `translating-capability-into-their-vocabulary-cfo` (per-stakeholder vocabulary within one deal) but goes further into this unit's own territory: the psychological gap between appreciation (admiring a capability) and conviction (a specific number, against a specific budget, worth acting on now), and the mechanics of building a credible, quantified ROI case that closes that gap — cost of inaction, cost of adoption, and the payback period derived from them. Confirmed `buyer-psychology` is complementary, not overlapping: that unit decodes a hesitation that's already surfaced; this unit proactively builds the case before a purchase decision.
- **Scenario throughline**: a finance director says "that's genuinely impressive" after a demo of an invoice-reconciliation tool and never follows up; three weeks later a different rep pitches the identical capability anchored to a real number ("saves about $48k a year, pays for itself in seven weeks") and closes within the week. L3 builds one full ROI case end to end with a real ledger (labor + rework costs sourced from the buyer's own numbers, an `xychart-beta` bar chart of where the monthly cost is going, an honest cost-of-adoption table including implementation effort), derives the payback period two ways (a naive full-rate division vs. the honest week-by-week balance accounting for a 3-week ramp-up at half savings — landing on the same "just under seven weeks" the L1 pitch uses), then contrasts that conservative, buyer-sourced case against a cautionary industry-benchmark pitch that promised $92k, delivered $30k, and cost the vendor credibility on every claim afterward even though the buyer stayed a customer. Two "what changes if..." extensions per rule 4b close L3: a skeptical buyer pushing back on a number, and a genuinely marginal (14-month) ROI case. All L3 math was hand-verified for internal consistency (the ledger, the two payback derivations, and the interactive's `compute` function all agree on the $3,988/mo cost of inaction → $2,900 one-time adoption cost → 7-week payback chain).
- `LevelIntro`×3, `Checkpoint`×4 (2 in L2 — whether "I understand this could help" counts as conviction, and why a vague claim stalls where a quantified one doesn't; 2 in L3 — why the honest, less-dramatic seven-week figure beats the optimistic four-and-a-half-week one, and whether the inflated pitch was still a mistake even though the buyer didn't leave), `Scenario`×1 (L1 only, per the pattern used when L2/L3 open with a bridging sentence into a guiding question instead), with a distinct `facts` slot (the two-pitch outcome comparison, not a copy of the narrative prose).

## 2026-09-04 — product-domain/objection-handling, full L1-L3/exercises/interactives

Wrote `product-domain/objection-handling` end-to-end: 'How do I handle "we already have something that works" or "it\'s too expensive"? (objection handling, negotiation)'. L1 and L2 had already been written and verified by a prior session that hit a session limit; this session picked up from there without touching either file, and wrote L3, `exercises.json`, `interactives.json`, and flipped the roadmap status.

- L1/L2 (from the prior session, unmodified) establish the acknowledge → isolate → reframe structure, a weak-vs-strong response comparison table across four objection types, and negotiation basics (concession trading, anchoring, walking away), with explicit cross-references to `buyer-psychology` (diagnosing the real hesitation) and `value-based-selling` (the reframe territory for price objections).
- **L3 scenario**: one deal, both canonical objections back to back. Dana (rep) and Marcus (ops manager at a logistics company) — the incumbent objection first (a spreadsheet double-booking trucks, costing ~$8,400/quarter in overtime, surfaced only once Dana acknowledges and isolates instead of arguing), then the price objection once Marcus is engaged ($24,000/year list quoted, "too expensive," isolate reveals a real $18,000 budget ceiling, Dana counter-anchors with the confirmed overtime cost rather than caving, and closes at $19,800/year locked for two years in exchange for the two-year term plus a future reference call — a real trade, not a unilateral discount). Contrasted against Theo, an untrained rep handling the identical two moments (argues the incumbent point-by-point and nearly loses the call, then caves to $18,000 flat with nothing received back) to show a similar final number can hide a very different, worse deal. Extended per rule 4b into what changes when the objection is a smokescreen for a different real blocker (approval authority, not the spreadsheet) and what changes when the budget constraint turns out to be genuinely frozen and non-negotiable — walking away, with the relationship left open, being the correct outcome rather than a failure of the structure.
- `LevelIntro`×1 (L3 only, this session's scope), `Checkpoint`×2 in L3 (why Dana's one-sentence reframe worked once Marcus had already supplied the problem and cost; why Dana's final $19,800 above Marcus's stated $18,000 ceiling still landed given isolate had confirmed that ceiling was real).

- `interactives.json`: one slider demo (`concession-trade-value`, L3) with two params (discount % given, contract term locked in years) grounded in Dana and Marcus's actual numbers ($24,000/year list, defaults at 17.5% discount / 2-year term reproduce Dana's real $19,800 outcome), computing an effective annual value that nets the discount against the value of price-lock protection over the term — single output line, verified by hand-checking the compute arithmetic at the default and both param extremes (0%/1yr, 40%/5yr) for sane, non-negative results.
- `exercises.json`: 40 items (13/13/14 across L1/L2/L3), 18 pools (6 per level), all `quiz` type per rule 9 (no natural code artifact in this soft-skill track). ## 2026-09-04 — product-domain/case-studies, full L1-L3/exercises/interactives — completes the product-domain track

Wrote `product-domain/case-studies` end-to-end: "How do I turn one client win into a repeatable opportunity? (case studies, referrals, compounding momentum)" — row 12, the last row in the `product-domain` track. The three empty `.md` stubs (already scaffolded by `new:unit`, `ROADMAP.md` row already `in-progress`) were deleted and replaced with fresh `.mdx` content.

- **Scope check against all 11 sibling units** (read each one's `L1-summary` first): confirmed this unit stays out of the pre-sale/domain-modeling territory (`understanding-user-s-problem-solution`, `requirements-gathering`, `domain-driven-design-basics`, `bounded-contexts`, `validation`), the general framing principle (`value-framing-over-feature-framing`), the live-conversation/negotiation techniques for winning one deal (`buyer-psychology`, `translating-capability-into-their-vocabulary-cfo`, `value-based-selling`, `discovery`, `objection-handling`). This unit is explicitly the compounding step _after_ a deal closes: turning one win into a documented case study and a well-timed, specifically-framed referral ask, and why that habit compounds pipeline quality over time rather than just adding one deal at a time.
- **Scenario throughline, deliberately continuing the track's own history rather than inventing an unrelated example**: L1 opens with two reps, Omar and Sana, closing an identical $21,000/year deal the same week — Omar starts the next cold-prospecting cycle immediately, Sana spends 20 minutes writing a one-page case study and making a specific referral ask; three months later Omar has 3 cold leads and nothing closed, Sana has 2 warm referrals with one already closed at $23,000. L2 builds the model: a weak-vs-strong case-study-elements table (numbers/voice/structure/credibility), a mermaid diagram of the win → case study → referral ask → warm lead → new win compounding loop, and the timing/framing mechanics behind a referral ask that lands. L3 picks up the exact deal `objection-handling` left off — Dana and Marcus at Fairmont Logistics, closed at $19,800/year locked for two years in exchange for a promised reference call "if the tool performs... after the first quarter" — and runs the real follow-up three months later: Dana confirms the actual result with Marcus (0 double-bookings vs. 4 the quarter before, the full $8,400/quarter avoided), writes the real one-page case-study document text (not a description of one), then the actual referral-ask dialogue that gets Marcus to name a specific contact (Renee at Castleview Freight) almost immediately. Contrasted against Theo (the track's recurring untrained-rep character) doing both badly — a vague, unattributed case study and a passive "if you know anyone" P.S. that gets silence despite an equally real win. Extended per rule 4b into what changes when the client will do private reference calls but won't go public by name, and what changes when the result is real but only modest (a 50% reduction, not 100%) — explicitly tying the honesty point back to `value-based-selling`'s $92k-promised/$30k-delivered cautionary tale — plus a closing prompt inviting the reader to extend the pattern beyond a cost-avoidance win to a deadline-hit or contract-retention win.
- `LevelIntro`×3, `Checkpoint`×4 (2 in L2 — why a specific, checkable number persuades more than vague praise even when both describe the same result, and what's lost when a referral ask is delayed to a renewal call even if the client still says yes; 2 in L3 — why the case study quotes Marcus's own informal phrasing instead of paraphrasing it, and why Theo's referral ask still failed despite an equally real, satisfied client), `Scenario`×1 (L1 only, per the pattern used across the track when L2/L3 open with a bridging sentence into a guiding question instead), with a distinct `facts` slot (an icon-row comparing Omar's and Sana's habit and three-month pipeline outcome, not a copy of the narrative prose).

- Visual elements: 1 mermaid diagram (L2: the win → case study → referral ask → warm lead → new win compounding loop), 3 tables (L2: the weak-vs-strong case-study-elements table; L3: the Dana-vs-Theo case-study/ask/outcome comparison table).
- `interactives.json`: one slider demo (`compounding-referral-pipeline`, L2) — four params (case studies written per quarter, referrals generated per case study, close rate on referred leads, time horizon in quarters) computing cumulative revenue from referred leads at a $20,000 average deal size (Sana's and Dana's range), extending L1's three-month gap out to a full year; at the defaults (1/quarter, 1.5 referrals each, 45% close rate, 4 quarters) it lands at $54,000 — the habit's own contribution, disappearing to $0 if any driving param is zeroed. ## 2026-09-04 — learning-craft/verification-habits, full L1-L3/exercises/interactives — first unit of the learning-craft track

Wrote `learning-craft/verification-habits` end-to-end: "How do I use AI/LLM tools to learn faster without atrophying my own judgment? (verification habits, not outsourcing understanding)" — row 08, the first unit written in the `learning-craft` track (3rd of 7 remaining tracks per `docs/HANDOFF.md`'s tracked order). The three empty `.md` stubs (already scaffolded by `new:unit`, `ROADMAP.md` row already `in-progress`) were deleted and replaced with fresh `.mdx` content.

- **Scope check against all 7 sibling units** (read each one's `L1-summary` first): confirmed this unit stays out of `exposure-understanding`'s territory (reading/consuming more ≠ learning, a general encoding-failure problem, not AI-specific), `source-evaluation` (evaluating a static published source), `search-literacy` (framing a search query), `feynman-technique` (self-testing your own recall — the illusion of competence about _your own_ understanding), `spaced-repetition` (retention over time), `systematic-exploration` (a codebase-reading technique), and `weighing-credibility` (weighing conflicting _human_ expert claims). This unit is specifically about the newer risk of a fluent, confident, plausible-sounding AI answer that is wrong or subtly incomplete, and the habit of verifying an AI's output _before_ it becomes part of what you believe — distinct from Feynman's self-recall test and from source-evaluation's static-document red flags (an LLM answer carries none of the usual unreliability tells and often reads _more_ authoritative than a shaky article, which is the actual danger).
- **Track classification (rule 4c)**: this specific unit sits at the edge of domain-general — grounded the throughline in a universal, non-engineering scenario (two parents, an infant, a food-safety question) rather than defaulting to an engineering scenario just because the site's audience is engineers; one engineering-specific illustrative aside (Python's `list.sort()` time complexity) appears only in L3, per the unit brief.
- **Scenario throughline**: Ravi and Ines, each with an 8-month-old, ask the same AI assistant the identical question the same evening ("is a spoonful of honey in oatmeal safe?") and get the identical fluent, confident, wrong answer (honey is fine past 6 months — actually unsafe under 12 months due to infant botulism risk). Ines has a habit of checking the one load-bearing claim against an independent, trusted source (her pediatrician's page) before acting — catches the error in thirty seconds and skips the honey. Ravi trusts the fluent answer and serves it, finding out the truth two days later from a worried forum comment. L2 builds the model: why next-token-prediction optimizes for plausible text, not verified text (a table contrasting passive acceptance vs. active verification at three specific moments, and a mermaid diagram of the outsourcing-understanding-trap loop vs. the AI-as-accelerant loop, which fork at the exact same decision point). L3 walks Ines's thirty-second check step by step as a real worked example (identify the load-bearing claim, pick a genuinely independent source rather than re-asking the same AI, compare directly, act on the checked version, close the loop), extends into a Python sort-complexity aside (the same failure mode in a technical domain, with a comparison table of easily-checkable vs. highly-technical claims), then extends further per rule 4b into what changes under real time pressure (compress the check, flag what's unverified — a lucky correct outcome doesn't validate skipping the flag) and closes with an explicit invitation for the reader to extend the pattern to a claim outside their own domain knowledge and to a two-minute decision window.
- `LevelIntro`×3, `Checkpoint`×4 (2 in L2 — whether a named citation in an AI's answer counts as verification, and whether the habit means checking every sentence with equal intensity vs. scaling to stakes; 2 in L3 — whether skipping a non-load-bearing detail was a gap in Ines's check, and whether a colleague's unflagged unverified claim turning out correct validates skipping the flag), `Scenario`×1 (L1 only, per the pattern used across the site when L2/L3 open with a bridging sentence into a guiding question instead), with a distinct `facts` slot (an icon list contrasting Ravi's and Ines's actions and outcomes, not a copy of the narrative prose).
- Rule 9: `learning-craft` is a technical/transversal track where the logic is naturally computational, but this unit's actual substance is judgment/reasoning about AI-answer reliability rather than an algorithm — L3 is grounded in fully worked prose scenarios (the honey walkthrough, the sort-complexity aside) rather than forced code, consistent with rule 9's carve-out for content whose real substance is reasoning, not an artifact to implement; no fake pseudocode anywhere.
- Visual elements: 1 mermaid diagram (L2: outsourcing-understanding-trap vs. AI-as-accelerant loop, forking at the same decision point), 3 tables (L2: passive-acceptance-vs-active-verification across three moments; L3: easily-checkable-vs-highly-technical-claim comparison), plus L1's `Scenario` `facts` icon-list contrast (not a chart, since the fact being conveyed is qualitative — who checked what and when — not a quantity).
- `interactives.json`: one slider demo (`verification-checks-vs-errors-caught`, L2) — a single param (independent verification checks performed, 0–5) computing the probability a genuine error gets caught via `1 - (1 - 0.65)^checks`, grounded in Ines's one-check habit (default 1 check → 65%), showing the diminishing-returns curve directly supporting L2's "scale scrutiny, don't verify everything with equal intensity" point (2 checks → 87.8%, 5 checks → 99.5%, marginal gain shrinking each step). Single output per the no-arithmetic-complement-pairing rule. Verified via `node -e` sweeping the full 0–5 range for the exact values above.
- `exercises.json`: 40 items (13/13/14 across L1/L2/L3), 15 pools (5 per level, 2-3 variants each), all `quiz` type — L3 is judgment-based (is this verification habit sufficient, what should be checked here) per the unit brief, since this isn't fundamentally a coding topic even though the track is technical. Verified via an inline Node structural script (run from the scratchpad, not left in the repo): 40/40 items present, exact 13/13/14 level split, all 15 pools internally consistent (single level, single type, identical `reference` HTML across variants), 0 missing explanations, 0 duplicate ids.
- Ran `bun run generate:roadmap` (18 tracks, 246 units parsed, `src/data/roadmap.json` and `ROADMAP.md` rewritten by Prettier) this session. Not run: `validate:content`/`lint`/`format:check`/`typecheck`/`test`/`build`/`astro preview` browser verification — left for the coordinating session to run.
- `ROADMAP.md` flipped `verification-habits` from `in-progress` → `done` — this is the first unit written in the `learning-craft` track (5 units remain: `notes`, `prioritization`, `curation`, `calibration`, `breadth-depth-trade-off-scale`).

## 2026-09-04 — learning-craft/notes, full L1-L3/exercises/interactives

Wrote `learning-craft/notes` end-to-end: "How do I build a personal system for capturing and resurfacing what I learn? (notes, spaced review, second-brain practices)" — row 09, second unit written in the `learning-craft` track. The three empty `.md` stubs (already scaffolded by `new:unit`, `ROADMAP.md` row already `in-progress`) were deleted and replaced with fresh `.mdx` content.

- **Scope check against all 7 sibling units** (read each one's `L1-summary` first): confirmed this unit stays out of `exposure-understanding`'s territory (reading more ≠ learning, an encoding problem), `source-evaluation` (trusting a source), `search-literacy` (framing a search query), `feynman-technique` (the teach-back test for whether you understood something), `spaced-repetition` (the review-timing/retention mechanism for a fixed set of discrete facts), `systematic-exploration` (learning an unfamiliar codebase), and `weighing-credibility` (evaluating conflicting expert claims). This unit is specifically the _system_ that captures and organizes messier, connected, evolving understanding so it's resurfaceable months later — the capture → process (own words) → link/organize → resurface loop, the capture-vs-organize friction trade-off, and the actual habits (own-words processing, cheap linking, deferred-but-not-skipped processing) that make a note system survive contact with reality rather than becoming a write-only graveyard.
- **Track classification (rule 4c)**: `learning-craft` is domain-general, and this unit is genuinely domain-general (a personal note system applies identically to a student, a doctor, an engineer) — used a simple, universal negotiation-course scenario rather than an engineering-forced one.
- **Scenario throughline**: Tom and Priya both take a negotiation course. Tom copies 45 pages of slides/highlights verbatim and never reopens the document; Priya writes six short own-words notes, each linked to 2–3 related notes. Six months later both hit a real negotiation where the course's anchoring idea would help — Priya follows a link trail from an unrelated note she was already reading and has the idea in front of her in two minutes; Tom searches his 45-page dump for "anchor," gets six noisy matches from an unrelated section, and gives up. L2 builds the model (a mermaid capture→process→link→resurface loop diagram, a copy-paste-vs-own-words comparison table on the same anchoring fact, and the capture-vs-organize friction trade-off). L3 walks the same anchoring note through all four steps in full — the four-second raw capture (`anchor first even if rough - Kessler??`), the three-minute own-words processing pass, the two links added (a thin one included deliberately), and the exact six-months-later resurfacing moment — compared side by side against Tom's raw dump failing to resurface anything, then extends per rule 4b into what changes for fast-moving/ephemeral information vs. foundational ideas worth the extra investment, and a triage rule for capturing under real time pressure (capture stays non-negotiable; processing can defer only if the raw fragment carries enough specific context to survive the delay).
- `LevelIntro`×3, `Checkpoint`×3 (2 in L2 — whether skipping a formal weekly review means resurfacing is missing; whether heavier real-time organization is automatically better than Priya's lighter system; 1 in L3 — whether the "thin" sunk-cost link was a mistake to add), `Scenario`×1 (L1 only, per the pattern used across the site when L2/L3 open with a bridging sentence into a guiding question instead), with a distinct `facts` slot (an icon list contrasting Tom's and Priya's habits and six-month outcome, not a copy of the narrative prose).
- No fake pseudocode: L3 is grounded entirely in a fully worked prose/dialogue scenario (the anchoring note walked through all four steps, the resurfacing moment, the triage extensions) — no fenced code blocks anywhere in the unit, consistent with the spirit of rule 9 even though `learning-craft` isn't in its explicit non-technical list, since this unit's real substance is habit/judgment, not an algorithm.
- Visual elements: 1 mermaid diagram (L2: the capture → process → link → resurface loop, with a "reinforces" back-edge from resurface to process), 2 tables (L2: copy-paste-vs-own-words comparison on the same anchoring fact; also referenced again via exercise `reference` panels), plus L1's `Scenario` `facts` icon-list contrast.
- `interactives.json`: one slider demo (`linking-minutes-vs-resurface-odds`, L2) — a single param (minutes spent processing/linking a note, 0–10) computing the odds of later finding/reusing it via a saturating curve (`95 * (1 - e^-0.5m)`), grounded in Priya's actual ~3-minute habit (default 3 min → 73.8%), showing strong diminishing returns past ~5 minutes (87.2% at 5 min, 94.4% at 10 min) — directly supporting L2's "fast, low-friction capture with lightweight processing beats heavy real-time filing" point. Single output per the no-arithmetic-complement-pairing rule. Verified via `node -e` sweeping the full 0–10 range for the exact values above.
- `exercises.json`: 40 items (13/13/14 across L1/L2/L3), 15 pools (5 per level, 2–3 variants each), all `quiz` type per the unit brief (no natural code artifact in this soft-skill-adjacent unit). ## 2026-09-04 — learning-craft/prioritization, full L1-L3/exercises/interactives

Wrote `learning-craft/prioritization` end-to-end: "How do I decide what's worth learning deeply vs. skimming? (prioritization, the cost of depth, just-in-time vs. just-in-case learning)" — row 10, third unit written in the `learning-craft` track. The three empty `.md` stubs (already scaffolded by `new:unit`, `ROADMAP.md` row already `in-progress`) were deleted and replaced with fresh `.mdx` content.

- **Scope check against all 9 sibling units** (read each one's `L1-summary` first): confirmed this unit stays out of `exposure-understanding` (reading/consuming more ≠ learning), `source-evaluation`/`search-literacy`/`weighing-credibility` (trusting and finding a specific source once you've decided to look), `feynman-technique` (testing your own recall of something you've already engaged with), `spaced-repetition` (retention over time for material already chosen), `systematic-exploration` (a codebase-reading technique), `verification-habits` (checking an AI's answer before believing it), and `notes` (capturing/organizing what you've already decided to learn). This unit sits upstream of all of those: the allocation decision of _what_ deserves deep learning effort at all versus a skim versus nothing right now, before any of those other techniques come into play.
- **Track classification (rule 4c)**: `learning-craft` is domain-general, and this unit is genuinely domain-general — used a simple, universal moving-to-a-new-country scenario rather than an engineering-forced one; no code appears anywhere in the unit (rule 9 carve-out, since the real substance is a decision heuristic, not an algorithm).
- **Scenario throughline**: Marco and Sofia each get 20 hours to prepare for a move to a new country, with the same six-topic list. Marco splits evenly (~3.3h/topic); Sofia concentrates 15 of her 20 hours on the two topics that are both likely to matter soon and costly to learn later (visa paperwork, opening a bank account), skimming the rest. Marco's visa paperwork bounces and he can't open a bank account for three weeks; Sofia's both sail through, and she picks up tipping customs free from a coworker. L2 builds the model: depth-isn't-free framing, the two-axis 2x2 table (likelihood-to-matter × cost-to-learn-later → deep now / just-in-time / skip / cheap-insurance-skim), a mermaid triage flowchart, and foundational-ness as a third factor that can override the other two. L3 runs the full triage on Sofia's real 9-item list with an explicit scored table, including a bet that paid off exactly as predicted (visa/banking) and one that went wrong (health insurance enrollment — she wrongly assumed low cost-to-learn-later by false analogy to tipping customs, missing a real 10-day enrollment window), her recovery from the miss with 3 days of runway left, and a re-triage once new information arrived (local tax filing moved from "skip" to "deep now"). Extended per rule 4b into what changes under much heavier time pressure (2 hours vs. 20 — the bar for "deep now" rises and even the "worth a skim" tier shrinks) and closes with an explicit prompt inviting the reader to extend the triage to a new-job scenario instead of a move.
- `LevelIntro`×3, `Checkpoint`×4 (2 in L2 — whether a low-likelihood/high-cost-to-learn-later item just needs a skim, and whether "it's foundational" alone justifies deep study regardless of everything else; 2 in L3 — whether the health-insurance miss means the triage _method_ failed, and whether the tax-filing item moving from "skip" to "deep now" means the original day-one call was wrong), `Scenario`×1 (L1 only, per the pattern used across the track when L2/L3 open with a bridging sentence into a guiding question instead), with a distinct `facts` slot (an icon-list contrast of Marco's and Sofia's hours and month-1 outcomes, not a copy of the narrative prose).
- Visual elements: 1 mermaid diagram (L2: the triage decision flowchart — likely to matter? → foundational? / cheap to learn later?), 3 tables (L1: Marco's vs. Sofia's hours per topic; L2: the 2x2 likelihood × cost-to-learn-later quadrant table; L3: the full 9-item scored triage table), plus L1's `Scenario` `facts` icon-list contrast.
- `interactives.json`: one slider demo (`depth-per-priority-topic`, L2) — two params (total prep hours, number of topics flagged "deep now") computing hours of depth available per priority topic as `(totalHours * 0.75) / numPriorityItems`, grounded in Sofia's actual numbers (default 20h / 2 topics reproduces her real 7.5h each on visa paperwork and banking) — directly illustrating L2's "depth isn't free" point: naming more topics "high priority" thins the depth each one actually gets. Single output per the no-arithmetic-complement-pairing rule. Verified via `node -e` sweeping totalHours (5/20/40) × numPriorityItems (1/2/6) for sane, monotonically-decreasing, non-negative results.
- `exercises.json`: 40 items (13/13/14 across L1/L2/L3), 15 pools (5 per level, 2-3 variants each), all `quiz` type per rule 9 (no natural code artifact in this domain-general decision-making unit). Verified via an inline Node structural script (run from the scratchpad, not left in the repo): 40/40 items, exact 13/13/14 level split, all 15 pools internally consistent (single level, single type, identical `reference` HTML across variants), 0 duplicate ids, 0 missing explanations.

## 2026-09-04 — learning-craft/curation, full L1-L3/exercises/interactives

Wrote `learning-craft/curation` end-to-end: "How do I keep up with a fast-moving field without drowning in noise? (curation, filtering, signal vs. hype)" — row 11, fourth unit written in the `learning-craft` track. The three empty `.md` stubs (already scaffolded by `new:unit`, `ROADMAP.md` row already `in-progress`) were deleted and replaced with fresh `.mdx` content.

- **Scope check against all 10 sibling units** (read each one's `L1-summary` first): confirmed this unit stays out of `exposure-understanding` (reading more ≠ learning once you've already chosen what to read), `source-evaluation`/`weighing-credibility` (evaluating whether one already-selected claim or source is trustworthy), `search-literacy` (framing a query to find a specific answer), `feynman-technique` (self-testing recall), `spaced-repetition` (retention over time), `systematic-exploration` (a codebase-reading technique), `verification-habits` (checking an AI's answer before believing it), `notes` (capturing/organizing what's already been decided worth keeping), and `prioritization` (deciding what's worth deep study once a candidate topic is already identified). This unit sits upstream of all of those: managing an ongoing, high-volume _stream_ of incoming information in a fast-moving field and deciding what even enters attention at all — the checking-constantly-vs-batching cost asymmetry, signal-vs-hype heuristics (recency bias, regression to the mean, track-record trust), and building an actual curation system (a small number of trusted filters instead of a raw firehose, batched review, a deliberate good-enough threshold).
- **Track classification (rule 4c)**: `learning-craft` is domain-general, and this unit is genuinely domain-general — used a simple, universal scenario (two people following nutrition science, a fast-moving field with lots of noise) rather than an engineering-forced one; no code appears anywhere in the unit (rule 9 carve-out, since the real substance is a filtering habit/system, not an algorithm).
- **Scenario throughline**: Elena follows 40+ raw sources/feeds in nutrition science and checks 6-8x/day (~10 hrs/week); Diego follows 3 trusted curators and reviews a weekly digest once, in one sitting (~1 hr/week). Over a month, Elena chases 5 "this changes everything" claims and 4 fizzle, leaving her confused about what's actually true despite 10x the hours; Diego's digest surfaces the 1 claim that held up and skips the rest. L2 builds the model: the volume-vs-attention mismatch (attention is fixed, a fast field's volume is effectively unbounded, so checking more doesn't close the gap), a mermaid funnel diagram (raw stream → trusted filters → batched review, most items dropped not delayed), and the three signal-vs-hype heuristics as a table (recency bias check, regression to the mean, track-record trust). L3 runs one full week through Diego's system in order: ~200 raw items reduced to a 4-line digest, what gets dropped outright (duplicate coverage, opinion, already-flagged bait), the item worth a 5-minute skim that doesn't pan out (a supplement pre-print — track-record/regression heuristics correctly flagged it as unproven), the item that turns out to matter (a fiber study, "consistent with three prior studies," confirmed by a meta-analysis two months later), and one of Diego's own curators retracting a two-month-old recommendation — showing even a good filter gets fooled sometimes, and the right response is a clean reversal, not abandoning curation. Extended per rule 4b into what changes with no trusted curators yet (a temporary wider observation period before pruning down) and what changes when missing something early has real time-sensitive cost (calibrate the batching interval to that field's actual cost of delay, not copy nutrition science's weekly default), closing with an explicit prompt to apply the same triage to a field the reader follows casually.
- `LevelIntro`×3, `Checkpoint`×4 (2 in L2 — whether the funnel's curators are just adding a delay to the same information Elena sees, and whether a brand-new, loud, no-track-record claim should be automatically dismissed as hype; 2 in L3 — whether the supplement pre-print's 5-minute skim was wasted time since Diego ultimately did nothing differently based on it, and whether the curator's retraction means the whole filtering system failed and Diego should go back to reading the raw stream himself), `Scenario`×1 (L1 only, per the pattern used across the track when L2/L3 open with a bridging sentence into a guiding question instead), with a distinct `facts` slot (an icon-list contrast of Elena's and Diego's hours, checking pattern, and month-end outcome, not a copy of the narrative prose).
- Visual elements: 1 mermaid diagram (L2: the raw stream → trusted filters → batched review funnel, showing most items dropped rather than merely delayed), 3 tables (L1: Elena-vs-Diego comparison table; L2: the three signal-vs-hype heuristics with what they say and why they work; L3 uses prose/dialogue for the worked week rather than a table, since the substance is a sequence of triage decisions, not a static comparison), plus L1's `Scenario` `facts` icon-list contrast.
- `interactives.json`: one slider demo (`raw-monitoring-hours-vs-useful-signal`, L2) — a single param (raw-feed monitoring hours per week, 0-15) computing genuinely-useful items surfaced via a saturating curve (`2 * (1 - e^-0.3h)`), grounded in Elena's actual ~10 hrs/week habit (default 10h → 1.9 of a ~2-item weekly ceiling), showing strong diminishing returns past ~5 hours (1.55 at 5h vs. 1.9 at 10h vs. 1.98 at 15h) — directly illustrating L2's volume-vs-attention mismatch: a fast field only produces a small, roughly fixed amount of genuine signal per week regardless of how much raw time is poured in. Single output per the no-arithmetic-complement-pairing rule. Verified via `node -e` sweeping the full 0-15 range for the exact values above.
- `exercises.json`: 40 items (13/13/14 across L1/L2/L3), 15 pools (5 per level, 2-3 variants each), all `quiz` type per rule 9 (no natural code artifact in this domain-general topic). Verified via an inline Python structural script (run from the scratchpad, not left in the repo): 40/40 items, exact 13/13/14 level split, all 15 pools internally consistent (single level, single type, identical `reference` HTML across variants), 0 duplicate ids, 0 missing explanations/references, all `correctIndex` values in range.
